That is why it is best to create it at the start of the project. And if you ever push a password, change it immediately: we cover this in chapter 10.

Cycle summary

git status                 # 1. What changed?
git diff                   # 2. What exactly did I change?
git add file               # 3. I choose what goes in
git diff --staged          #    I review the box
git commit -m "Message"    # 4. I save with a good message
git log --oneline          # 5. I look at the history
git pull                   # 6. I bring in what is new
git push                   #    I publish

Choosing parts of a file: git add -p

Sometimes you changed two different things in the same file: a correction and a test. With git add file both would go in. To choose piece by piece:

git add -p README.md

Git shows you each block of changes and asks what to do. The answers you will use most:

  • y (yes): this block goes into the box.

  • n (no): this block stays out.

  • q (quit): ends without reviewing the rest.

  • ? (help): shows all the options.

It is the perfect tool to follow the "one commit, one idea" rule. In VS Code and Cursor you can do the same with the mouse: select lines in the changes view and choose Stage Selected Ranges.

Viewing a commit in detail: git show

git show a3f9c21

It shows the author, the date, the message, and exactly which lines that commit changed. If you don't give a code, it shows the last commit. It is useful to answer "what did I do yesterday?" or "what did Ana change in that commit?".

Other uses of git log that will help you:

git log --oneline -5              # Only the last 5 commits
git log --oneline --author="Ana"  # Only Ana's commits
git log --oneline -- README.md    # Only the commits that touched that file

I made a mistake in the last commit (and I haven't pushed)

If you wrote the message wrong or forgot to add a file, you can fix the last commit:

git add forgotten-file.txt
git commit --amend -m "Corrected message"

Warning: use --amend only if you have not pushed yet. If you already published the commit, correcting it this way changes the history your teammates already downloaded and causes them problems. In that case, make a new commit with the correction.

How often should I commit?

There is no magic number, but these signals help:

  • Commit when you finish a complete idea, even a small one: you fixed a mistake, added a section, changed a text.

  • Commit before something risky, like reorganizing folders. If it goes wrong, you return to that point.

  • Don't wait for days. If you have gone a week without a commit, you have too many mixed changes and they will be hard to explain or undo.

  • Don't commit something broken to the main branch. If you need to save something half-finished, do it on your own branch (chapter 4).

A good test: if you can describe the change in one clear line, it is a good time to commit.

Common cycle errors and how to get out of them

What you seeWhat happenedWhat to do
nothing to commit, working tree cleanThere are no unsaved changesNothing, everything is saved. If you expected changes, check that you saved the file in your editor
nothing added to commitYou committed without adding anything to the boxUse git add first
rejected ... fetch first when pushingSomeone published before youRun git pull and then git push
A strange screen opened when committingGit opened an editor for the messageIn nano: type, Ctrl + O, Enter, Ctrl + X. In Vim: press i, type, then Esc, :wq and Enter
Your branch is ahead of 'origin/main' by 2 commitsYou have unpublished commitsRun git push
Your branch is behind 'origin/main'There are new changes on GitLabRun git pull

The same cycle from VS Code or Cursor

Everything in this chapter can also be done with buttons:

  1. Open the source control panel. That is your git status: the list of changed files.

  2. Click a file to see its git diff side by side.

  3. Press the + next to the file to put it in the box (git add).

  4. Write the message at the top and press Commit.

  5. Press Sync Changes or the arrow buttons to pull and push.

Be careful with the commit button when nothing is in the box: some editors offer to save all changes at once. It is the equivalent of git add . without looking. Read the message before accepting.

Real example: a clean commit versus a tangled one

Luis fixes a bug in the contact form. Meanwhile, he also changes some colors to test and leaves a test file in the folder.

Tangled commit. Luis uses git add . and writes git commit -m "fixes". Everything goes in together: the correction, the test colors and the junk file. Two weeks later, the team wants to undo the colors. They can't do it without also undoing the correction.

Clean commit. Luis looks at git status and git diff. He sees the three changes. He adds only the form file and runs git commit -m "Fix email validation in the contact form". He discards the test colors and adds the test file to .gitignore. The history is clear and each change can be undone separately.

Practical exercise

Use your practice-git project.

  1. Open README.md and add a line with your name and today's date.

  2. Create a new file called personal-notes.txt with any text.

  3. Run git status and explain in your own words what you see.

  4. Run git diff and find your new line with the +.

  5. Create a .gitignore file and write personal-notes.txt inside it. Run git status again: your notes should no longer appear.

  6. Add README.md and .gitignore by name. Review the box with git diff --staged.

  7. Make a commit with a clear message, for example: Add my name to the README and ignore personal notes.

  8. Run git log --oneline, then git pull and git push.

  9. Open GitLab in the browser and find your commit in the history.

Chapter checklist

  • I use git status before and after each step.

  • I review my changes with git diff before saving them.

  • I add files by name, and only use git add . after looking.

  • I know how to take a file out of the box with git restore --staged.

  • I write commit messages that start with a verb and say what changes.

  • I make one commit per idea.

  • I run git pull before git push.

  • I have a .gitignore and I know what it is for.

  • I published my first change and saw it in GitLab.

PreviousChapter 2. Set Up Your ComputerNextChapter 4. Branches: Work Without Fear

Chapter 4

Branches: Work Without Fear

A branch is a parallel working copy inside the same project. It lets you try things, make mistakes and finish a change without touching the main version, which is the one the whole team uses. If you learn only one habit from this chapter, let it be this one: never work directly on main.

Why branches exist

Imagine your team maintains the office website. The version on main is the one that works and the one that gets published. Now you are asked to redesign the contact form, which will take you three days.

If you work directly on main:

  • For three days, the main version has a half-finished form.

  • If in the meantime someone needs to fix an urgent bug, they would have to publish your unfinished work too.

  • If halfway through you decide your idea doesn't work, undoing it is complicated.

With a branch, you do the whole redesign separately. main stays intact and stable. When you finish and someone reviews your work, you combine it with main. If your idea didn't work, you delete the branch and nobody ever knew.

A comparison: the draft

Think of main as the official document that has been published. A branch is a draft that you take from that document. You write, cross out and rewrite whatever you want in your draft. The official document doesn't change.

When your draft is ready, you ask for it to be reviewed. If it is approved, its changes go into the official document. That is called merging.

And the best part: each person on the team can have their own drafts at the same time, each one for a different task.

What a branch looks like inside

Remember from chapter 1 that each commit points to the previous one, forming a chain. A branch is simply a label that points to the latest commit of a line of work. When you make a commit on that branch, the label moves forward.

main:            A --- B --- C
                        \
contact-form:            D --- E

Here, main has commits A, B and C. The branch contact-form left from commit B and has its own commits, D and E. The two lines move forward without getting in each other's way. When you merge, the changes from D and E enter main.

That is why creating a branch is instant and takes no space: Git doesn't copy files, it just creates a new label.

Branch commands

See your branches

git branch

It shows your local branches. The one with an asterisk (*) is the one you are on now. To also see the branches that exist in GitLab:

git branch -a

The ones that start with remotes/origin/ are the GitLab ones.

Create a branch and switch to it

First, make sure you start from the latest version of main:

git switch main
git pull

Now create your branch and enter it with a single command:

git switch -c feature/contact-form

The -c means create. From this moment on, all your commits go to this branch.

And git checkout? In older tutorials you will see git checkout -b branch-name. It does the same thing. git switch is the modern and clearer way, because checkout does too many different things. In this book we use switch.

Switch between branches

git switch main
git switch feature/contact-form

When you switch branches, the files in your folder change to show the state of that branch. Don't be alarmed if a file "disappears": it is still saved in the other branch.

Publish your branch to GitLab

The first time you push a new branch:

git push -u origin feature/contact-form

After that first time, git push is enough. Publishing your branch has two advantages: your work is backed up on GitLab and your teammates can see it.

Keep your branch up to date with main

While you work on your branch, others keep merging changes into main. If your branch falls behind for a long time, joining it at the end will be harder. That is why it is good to bring the new changes from main into your branch every so often:

git switch main
git pull
git switch feature/contact-form
git merge main

This brings the new changes from main into your branch. If nobody touched the same things you did, it combines on its own. If there were changes on the same lines, a conflict will appear, and we solve it in chapter 6. It is better to solve a small conflict today than a huge one in a month.

You will hear about rebase. It is another way to update your branch that leaves a more linear history. It is useful, but it carries more risks for beginners. Stick with merge until you feel confident with everything in this book.

Delete a branch

When your branch has already been merged into main, you no longer need it:

git switch main
git branch -d feature/contact-form

If Git refuses with a message like not fully merged, it is protecting you: that branch has commits that are not in main. Check before deleting. If you are completely sure you want to throw that work away, use -D in capitals. Use it with great care.

To delete the branch on GitLab:

git push origin --delete feature/contact-form

Usually it is not necessary: GitLab can delete it automatically when you complete the Merge Request (we see this in chapter 5).

What happens to unsaved changes when you switch branches

This is a very common question. If you have modified files without a commit and switch branches, one of two things can happen:

  1. The changes travel with you to the new branch, if they don't clash with anything. This is confusing, because it looks as if the change "moved" to another branch.

  2. Git won't let you switch and tells you your changes would be overwritten. It is protecting you.

The simple rule: before switching branches, leave your folder clean. Commit what you have, or save it temporarily with git stash (we see it in chapter 7). Always check with git status before switching.

How to name branches

A good name says what kind of change it is and what it is about. The most used convention is type/short-description:

PrefixWhat forExample
feature/Something newfeature/contact-form
fix/Fixing a bugfix/footer-phone
hotfix/Urgent fix for productionhotfix/login-crash
docs/Documentation onlydocs/install-instructions
refactor/Reorganizing without changing what it doesrefactor/folder-structure
chore/Maintenance, configurationchore/update-dependencies

Rules for the name:

  • Everything in lowercase.

  • Words separated with hyphens, no spaces.

  • No accents or special letters: write installation, not instalación.

  • Short but clear: three or four words are enough.

  • If you work with issues (chapter 8), include their number: fix/42-footer-phone. That way anyone knows which task it belongs to.

The most important thing is that the whole team uses the same convention. If your team already has one, follow it even if it is different from this one.

Short-lived branches: the rule I learned the hard way

A branch should live days, not weeks. The longer it lives apart from main, the more changes both accumulate, and the harder it is to combine them. In chapter 6 I tell you about the huge conflict I lived through by not respecting this rule.

How to keep branches short:

  • Split big work into small pieces. Instead of one branch called "full website redesign", make one for the header, another for the form, another for the footer.

  • Merge often. Each finished and reviewed piece goes into main.

  • Bring main into your branch frequently if your work drags on.

  • One branch, one task. Don't use the form branch to "while I'm at it" fix something else.

Branches in GitLab

In the menu Code > Branches you see all the branches of the project. For each one, GitLab shows you:

  • Its last commit and who made it.

  • How many commits it is ahead of main (changes it has and main doesn't) and how many behind (changes from main it is missing).

  • A button to create a Merge Request.

  • Whether it is protected (chapter 10).

You can also create branches from there with New branch, although then you will need to bring it to your computer:

git fetch
git switch branch-name

git fetch brings the new information from GitLab without combining it with your work. It is like checking the mailbox without opening the letters.

Seeing branches as a drawing

To view the history with its branches drawn in the terminal:

git log --oneline --graph --all

You will see lines and asterisks that show where each branch split off and where it merged back. At first it looks like a tangle, but with practice it reads like a subway map. In GitLab you have the same view, prettier, under Code > Repository graph.

Common branch mistakes

What happenedHow to avoid or fix it
I made commits on main instead of my branchSee chapter 7, "I committed to the wrong branch". Prevention: always check the branch with git status
I created the branch from an old version of mainBefore creating the branch, run git switch main and git pull
error: pathspec 'name' did not matchThe branch doesn't exist locally. Run git fetch and check the exact name with git branch -a
I don't know which branch I'm ongit status says it on the first line. In the editor, look at the bottom left
My branch has things from another taskOne branch per task. Move those changes to their own branch before asking for review

Real example: an urgent bug in the middle of your work

Ana is on the branch feature/contact-form, halfway through the redesign. Suddenly she is told that the phone number in the footer is wrong and it has to be fixed today.

  1. Ana commits what she has on her branch: git commit -m "Progress: structure of the new form".

  2. She goes back to main and updates it: git switch main and git pull.

  3. She creates a branch for the fix: git switch -c fix/footer-phone.

  4. She corrects the phone number, commits, pushes, and asks for review.

  5. The fix is merged into main and published the same day.

  6. Ana returns to her branch: git switch feature/contact-form.

  7. She brings the fix into her branch: git merge main.

The urgent bug was resolved without publishing the half-finished form. And Ana's branch is up to date. This is impossible to do cleanly if everyone works on main.

Practical exercise

Use your practice-git project.

  1. Make sure you are on main and up to date: git switch main and git pull.

  2. Create a branch called feature/about-me-section.

  3. Create a file about-me.md with three lines about yourself. Commit it.

  4. Add one more line and make another commit.

  5. Publish your branch with git push -u origin feature/about-me-section.

  6. Go back to main with git switch main and look for the file about-me.md with ls. It isn't there. Go back to your branch: it is there.

  7. Open GitLab, go to Code > Branches and find your branch. How many commits is it ahead of main?

  8. Run git log --oneline --graph --all and find your two commits.

  9. Extra challenge: from main, create fix/readme-typo, correct or add something in the README, commit and push. Now you have two independent branches.

Don't delete these branches yet: you will use them in chapter 5.

Chapter checklist

  • I can explain what a branch is in my own words.

  • I know why you don't work directly on main.

  • Before creating a branch, I update main with git pull.

  • I know how to create a branch and switch with git switch -c and git switch.

  • I know how to publish a new branch with git push -u origin.

  • I know how to bring changes from main into my branch with git merge main.

  • I leave my folder clean before switching branches.

  • I name my branches type/short-description, without accents or spaces.

  • I keep my branches short: days, not weeks.

  • I know how to delete a branch and I understand why Git sometimes stops me.

  • I found my branches in GitLab and saw how many commits they are ahead.

PreviousChapter 3. Your First Cycle: Look Before You SaveNextChapter 5. Merge Requests: Ask Permission to Integrate

Chapter 5

Merge Requests: Ask Permission to Integrate

A Merge Request is a formal request to combine your branch with main. It is the moment when another person looks at your work before it enters the version the whole team uses. Here you will learn to create it, describe it well, receive comments without taking them personally, and complete the merge.

On GitHub it is called… a Pull Request (PR). It works exactly the same. When you hear "open a PR" or "open an MR", it is the same thing.

Why not simply merge

Technically you could combine your branch with main from the terminal, without asking anyone. But you would lose three very valuable things:

  1. Another pair of eyes. Whoever writes a change almost never sees their own mistakes. Someone who arrives fresh finds the detail you missed.

  2. A record of the decision. The Merge Request keeps what changed, why, who reviewed it and what was discussed. A year from now, someone can understand why the system works the way it does.

  3. Team learning. By reviewing other people's work you learn how they think and get to know parts of the project you don't touch.

In many teams, moreover, main is protected: nobody can merge without an approved Merge Request. We look at this in chapter 10.

If you want to go deeper into how to review code well, I have a book dedicated only to that. Here we focus on the process inside GitLab.

The full path of a Merge Request

  1. You work on your branch and push.

  2. You open the Merge Request: "I want to merge feature/x into main".

  3. You assign one or more people to review it.

  4. They review it and leave comments.

  5. You respond and fix things with new commits on the same branch.

  6. They approve.

  7. It is merged and the branch is deleted.

  8. Everyone updates their main with git pull.

Let's go step by step.

Step 1: create the Merge Request

There are three ways, all of them lead to the same form:

From the terminal. When you push a new branch, GitLab answers with a link like this one:

remote: To create a merge request for feature/contact-form, visit:
remote:   https://gitlab.com/your-username/project/-/merge_requests/new?...

Copy the link and open it in the browser.

From the GitLab notice. When you open the project after a push, GitLab shows a notice with your branch and a Create merge request button.

From the menu. Go to Code > Merge requests > New merge request. Choose your branch as the source (source branch) and main as the target (target branch).

Always check the source and the target before going on. A common mistake is opening the Merge Request toward the wrong branch.

Step 2: describe the change well

This is the part people neglect most and the one that helps the reviewer the most. A good description saves your teammate from guessing what you did.

The title. Like a good commit: start with a verb and say what the change does.

Confusing titleClear title
Form changesAdd email validation to the contact form
fixFix wrong phone number in the footer
Ana's branchUpdate installation instructions for Windows

The description. Use this template. You can copy it as it is:

## What changes
Adds email validation to the contact form.
Before, it was possible to send it with a misspelled email.

## Why
We were receiving messages we could not reply to.
Related to issue #42.

## How to test it
1. Open the contact page.
2. Type an email without @ and press Send.
3. The message "Invalid email" should appear.

## Screenshots
(before / after, if applicable)

## Pending or doubts
I did not validate specific domains. Is it needed?

Not every section always applies. But what changes, why, and how to test it should be in almost every Merge Request.

If you write Closes #42 in the description, GitLab will close issue 42 automatically when it is merged. We see this in chapter 8.

Step 3: the rest of the form

FieldWhat it isRecommendation
AssigneeThe person responsible for the Merge RequestUsually you
ReviewerWho should review itSomeone who knows that part of the project
LabelsTags to classify itThe ones your team uses: bug, documentation, etc.
MilestoneWhich delivery it belongs toIf your team works in deliveries
Delete source branchDelete your branch when mergingCheck it. It avoids old branches piling up
Squash commitsCombine all your commits into one when mergingDepends on your team's habit

What is squash? If your branch has ten commits like "progress", "more progress", "fix typo", squash turns them into one clean commit when it enters main. That way the main history stays tidy. Some teams always use it; others prefer to keep every commit.

Merge Requests in draft

If your work is not finished but you want to show it or ask for an early opinion, mark it as a draft (Draft). You can do it with the Mark as draft checkbox or by starting the title with Draft:.

A draft Merge Request cannot be merged. It is a clear way of saying: "it is not ready yet, but take a look". When you finish, remove the mark with Mark as ready.

Opening a Merge Request early, as a draft, is a very good practice. You get feedback before going too far in a wrong direction.

Step 4: review your own Merge Request first

Before asking someone to review, review it yourself. Go to the Changes tab and read your changes as if they were someone else's. Look for:

  • Files that shouldn't be there.

  • Test text, forgotten comments or unfinished things.

  • Spelling mistakes in texts the user will see.

  • Changes that have nothing to do with the task.

You will be surprised how many things you find. And each one you fix yourself is one your teammate doesn't have to point out.

How to review in GitLab (if you are asked to review)

When someone assigns you as a reviewer:

  1. Read the description first. Understand what it is trying to solve before looking at the changes.

  2. Open the Changes tab. You will see each file with added lines in green and removed lines in red.

  3. Comment on a line. Hover over the line number and click the comment icon. Write your observation.

  4. Suggest a concrete change. In the comment, use the Insert suggestion button. You write how the line should look and the author can apply it with one click.

  5. Group your comments. Use Start a review instead of Add comment now. That way your comments are published together at the end, instead of sending the author ten notifications.

  6. Decide. If everything is fine, press Approve. If there are things to correct, submit your review and wait for the changes.

How to write good comments

The review is about the code, not the person. A few guidelines:

Instead ofTry
"This is wrong.""Here we need to validate that the date is not empty. If it arrives empty, the form fails."
"Why did you do it this way?""What led you to this approach? I ask because X could be simpler."
"Change the name.""Suggestion: monthly_total would be clearer than tm."
(silence about what is good)"I liked how you separated this part, it reads very clearly."

It also helps to mark how important each comment is:

  • Blocking: it must be fixed before merging.

  • Suggestion: it would improve things, but it is not mandatory.

  • Nit (in Spanish you will see detalle): something minor, like a space or a word.

  • Question: it is not a request for a change, you just want to understand.

Step 5: respond to feedback

Receiving comments about your work can feel uncomfortable at first. Remember that review improves the result and protects you: it is better for a teammate to see the mistake today than for a user to see it tomorrow.

For each comment:

  1. Read it in full and make sure you understand it. If not, ask.

  2. If you agree, make the fix in your branch, on your computer:

git switch feature/contact-form
# you make the fix
git add fixed-file
git commit -m "Validate that the date is not empty"
git push

You don't need to open a new Merge Request. When you push to the same branch, the Merge Request updates itself with your new commits.

  1. Reply in the thread with something brief: "Fixed in the latest commit". If the reviewer used a suggestion, you can apply it directly with Apply suggestion.

  2. If you disagree, explain your reason respectfully. A good technical discussion is healthy. If you can't agree, ask for a third opinion.

  3. Mark the thread as resolved (Resolve thread) when it has been addressed. Some teams prefer that the person who wrote the comment resolves it: follow your team's habit.

  4. Let them know it is ready for another review. In GitLab you can request the review again with the icon next to the reviewer's name.

Step 6: complete the merge

When the Merge Request is ready, you will see the Merge button. GitLab may require some conditions before enabling it:

  • The necessary approvals.

  • All comment threads resolved.

  • The pipeline green (chapter 9).

  • No conflicts with main.

If everything is met, press Merge. Your changes enter main.

If GitLab says there are conflicts, you have to resolve them first. Chapter 6 shows you how.

Step 7: after the merge

Your branch is now in main, but your computer doesn't know it yet. Bring everything up to date:

git switch main
git pull
git branch -d feature/contact-form

Over time, your computer accumulates references to branches that were already deleted in GitLab. To clean them up:

git fetch --prune

What makes a good Merge Request big or small

Small Merge Requests are reviewed better and faster. One of 50 lines is reviewed carefully in ten minutes. One of 2,000 lines is hardly ever really reviewed: it gets approved out of exhaustion.

  • One Merge Request, one task. Same as branches.

  • If it is big, split it. Several small Merge Requests that are merged one after another.

  • Separate the mechanical from the important. If you renamed many files and also changed the logic, make two Merge Requests. That way the reviewer can focus on what matters.

Common Merge Request mistakes

What happenedHow to avoid or fix it
I opened it toward the wrong branchEdit the Merge Request and change the target branch
My new commits don't appearCheck that you pushed to the same branch
I opened a new Merge Request for every fixNot needed: pushing to the same branch updates the existing one
Nobody reviews my Merge RequestAssign an explicit reviewer and let them know in the team channel
Changes appear that I didn't makeYour branch came from an old version or from another branch. Check with git log and, if needed, create a new branch from an updated main

Real example: a Merge Request that improves thanks to review

Luis opens the Merge Request Add email validation to the contact form. In the description he explains what changes, why, and how to test it.

Ana reviews it. She leaves three comments: one blocking (the error message doesn't appear if the field is empty), one suggestion (a clearer function name, with the suggestion button) and one nit (a missing accent in a text).

Luis applies the suggestion with one click, fixes the empty field and the accent in a new commit, pushes and replies in each thread. Ana tests again, resolves the threads and approves. Luis merges, and the branch deletes itself.

Result: the empty-field bug never reached users. And there is a record of everything in GitLab.

Practical exercise

Use the branches you created in chapter 4. If you can, do it with another person as the reviewer; if not, play both roles yourself.

  1. Open a Merge Request from feature/about-me-section into main.

  2. Write a clear title and use the description template.

  3. Mark it as a draft. Then remove the mark with Mark as ready.

  4. Check the box to delete the branch when merging.

  5. Review it yourself first in the Changes tab.

  6. Leave a comment on one line, as if you were the reviewer. Use Insert suggestion to propose a change.

  7. Apply the suggestion, or fix it on your computer, commit and push. Verify that the Merge Request was updated.

  8. Reply to the comment and mark the thread as resolved.

  9. Merge.

  10. On your computer: git switch main, git pull and git branch -d feature/about-me-section.

  11. Repeat with the branch fix/readme-typo, this time trying the squash option.

Chapter checklist

  • I know what a Merge Request is and why you shouldn't merge without review.

  • I know how to create it from the push link, from the notice, or from the menu.

  • I always check the source branch and the target branch.

  • I write clear titles and descriptions covering what changes, why, and how to test it.

  • I know how to use draft mode.

  • I review my own Merge Request before asking for review.

  • I know how to comment on a line and propose a suggestion.

  • I write comments about the code, not about the person.

  • I know that a push to the same branch updates the Merge Request.

  • I respond to and resolve comment threads.

  • After the merge, I update main and delete my local branch.

  • I prefer small Merge Requests, one per task.

PreviousChapter 4. Branches: Work Without FearNextChapter 6. Conflicts: Don't Erase Someone Else's Work

Chapter 6

Conflicts: Don't Erase Someone Else's Work

A conflict appears when two people changed the same part of a file and Git can't decide which version is the right one. It is not an error or a failure: it is Git being honest and asking you for help. In this chapter you will lose your fear of conflicts and learn to resolve them without erasing anyone's work.

My story: the giant conflict

The worst conflict I ever lived through was not caused by a complicated change. It was caused by keeping two branches apart for weeks. While I was moving forward on mine, main kept changing too. When I finally wanted to combine them, Git marked conflicts in file after file.

Each conflict on its own was simple. The problem was the quantity, and that I no longer remembered well why I had made each change weeks earlier. Joining the branches took me far longer than doing the original work.

That is where the rule I gave you in chapter 4 came from: short branches, integrate often. A small conflict today takes five minutes to resolve. Twenty accumulated conflicts can cost you a whole day.

What I see in others: resolving by deleting

As an instructor, and on the teams where I have worked, I have seen the same scene many times. The conflict appears, the person gets scared and picks the fastest way out: keep their own version and discard the other one without reading it.

The conflict disappears from the screen. Git is happy. But a teammate's change has just been lost in silence. Nobody finds out until days later, when something that was already fixed breaks again.

That is why this chapter has one golden rule: a conflict is resolved by understanding both changes, not by picking yours by default.

Why conflicts appear

Git is very good at combining changes. If Ana changes line 10 of a file and Luis changes line 50, Git combines both without asking. If Ana changes index.html and Luis changes contact.html, there is no problem either.

A conflict only appears in these cases:

SituationExample
Two people change the same linesAna sets the hours until 5:00 and Luis sets them until 4:30
One person deletes a file and another modifies itLuis deleted old.md and Ana added a paragraph to it
Two people create a file with the same nameBoth added guide.md with different content

In all those cases there are two human decisions that clash. Git can't guess which one is right, and it is right not to guess.

When a conflict can show up

  • When you run git pull, if someone published changes to the same lines you changed.

  • When you run git merge main on your branch, to update it.

  • In a Merge Request, when GitLab warns that your branch conflicts with main and won't let you merge.

In every case it is resolved the same way. Let's see how.

What a conflict looks like

When there is a conflict, Git warns you in the terminal:

Auto-merging contact.md
CONFLICT (content): Merge conflict in contact.md
Automatic merge failed; fix conflicts and then commit the result.

And if you look at the status:

git status
You have unmerged paths.
  (fix conflicts and run "git commit")
  (use "git merge --abort" to abort the merge)

Unmerged paths:
        both modified:   contact.md

both modified means "both sides changed this file". That is the one you have to review.

When you open contact.md you will see something like this:

# Contact

<<<<<<< HEAD
Hours: Monday to Friday, 8:00 to 5:00
=======
Hours: Monday to Friday, 8:00 to 4:30
>>>>>>> fix/friday-hours

Phone: 500-0000

Those odd markers were put there by Git to show you both sides:

  • Between <<<<<<< HEAD and ======= is your version, the one on the branch you are on.

  • Between ======= and >>>>>>> fix/friday-hours is the version you are bringing in, from the other branch.

  • Everything outside the markers (like the phone number) has no conflict.

How to resolve it, step by step

1. Don't panic, and don't delete anything yet

The conflict hasn't broken anything. Your work and your teammate's work are safe. If you need to step away and think, you can cancel everything and return to how it was before:

git merge --abort

It is your emergency button. Use it without guilt.

2. Understand both changes

Before touching the file, ask yourself:

  • What was my change trying to do?

  • What was the other change trying to do?

  • Are they incompatible, or can they be combined?

To find out who made the other change and why:

git log --oneline main -- contact.md

Read the commit message. If it is still unclear, ask that person. A quick message like "Hey, we both changed the hours, which one is correct?" prevents losing work. This is the part people skip most often, and the most important one.

3. Decide how it should look

You have four options:

OptionWhenResult in the example
Keep your versionThe other change is obsolete, and you confirmed it8:00 to 5:00
Keep the other versionTheir change is the correct one8:00 to 4:30
Keep bothBoth lines should existBoth lines, one below the other
CombineEach change has a correct partFor example: Monday to Thursday 8:00 to 5:00, Friday 8:00 to 4:30

In real life, the right option is often combining. Rarely is one of the two changes completely unnecessary.

4. Edit the file and remove the markers

Leave the text exactly as it should be and delete the three markers (<<<<<<<, ======= and >>>>>>>). For example:

# Contact

Hours: Monday to Thursday 8:00 to 5:00, Friday 8:00 to 4:30

Phone: 500-0000

A very common mistake is forgetting one of the markers. Before going on, search the file for <<<<<<<. There should be none left.

5. Mark the conflict as resolved and save

git add contact.md
git status

When git status no longer shows files in conflict:

git commit

Git opens the editor with a message already written, such as Merge branch 'main' into fix/friday-hours. You can leave it like that or add a line explaining how you resolved it. Save and close. Then run git push.

6. Check that everything works

Resolving a conflict means changing code. Open the result, test it and make sure it does what both changes were meant to do. If the project has automatic tests, the pipeline will help you (chapter 9).

Resolving conflicts in VS Code or Cursor

The editor makes all of this more visual. When you open a file with a conflict you will see the two blocks colored, and above them some links:

  • Accept Current Change: keep your version.

  • Accept Incoming Change: keep the version that is coming in.

  • Accept Both Changes: keep both.

  • Compare Changes: see them side by side.

You can also use the Resolve in Merge Editor button, which shows three panels: your version, the incoming one, and the final result at the bottom. You can tick boxes and edit the result by hand.

Be careful: these buttons are convenient, but they make it easy to make the "accept mine" mistake without reading. Read both blocks before clicking. When you are done, save the file and continue with git add and git commit, or use the buttons in the Git panel.

Resolving conflicts in GitLab

If a Merge Request has simple conflicts, GitLab shows a Resolve conflicts button. It lets you choose, block by block, between your version (ours) and the other one (theirs), or edit the file directly in the browser.

It is useful for small text conflicts. For larger conflicts, or if you need to test the result, it is better to resolve them on your computer:

git switch main
git pull
git switch your-branch
git merge main
# you resolve the conflicts
git add resolved-files
git commit
git push

When you push, the Merge Request updates and the conflict warning disappears.

Special cases

One side deleted the file and the other modified it. git status will say deleted by them or deleted by us. Decide whether the file should exist. If it should stay, use git add file. If it should be deleted, use git rm file. Before deleting it, check whether the other side's change should have been moved somewhere else.

Conflicts in files that are not text (images, Word, PDF). Git can't show you the differences inside. You have to choose a complete version:

git checkout --ours logo.png     # I keep my version
git checkout --theirs logo.png   # I keep the other one
git add logo.png

Talk to the other person before choosing. One of you is going to lose their version.

Lots of conflicts at once. If it happens to you what happened to me, don't try to resolve everything in one go. Run git merge --abort and ask for help: the person who made the other changes, or someone with more experience. Sometimes it is better to integrate the changes in parts, or to create a new branch from an updated main and bring your changes in gradually.

How to prevent conflicts

You can't avoid all of them, and it is not bad that they appear. But you can make them few and small:

  • Short branches. Days, not weeks.

  • Bring main into your branch often if your work drags on.

  • git pull before you start working each day.

  • Small commits and Merge Requests. Fewer lines touched, fewer possible clashes.

  • Coordinate with your team. If two people are going to touch the same file, they should know. One line in the team chat saves hours.

  • Don't reformat whole files (changing spaces, sorting lines) mixed with real changes. It creates conflicts across the whole file. If needed, do it in a separate Merge Request.

Common mistakes when resolving conflicts

MistakeConsequenceHow to avoid it
Always accepting "my version"The other person's work is silently lostRead both blocks and ask if you're unsure
Leaving <<<<<<< markers in the fileThe file is brokenSearch for the markers before running git add
Not testing after resolvingTwo changes that don't work together get combinedAlways test the result
Resolving in a panic, without understandingErrors that are hard to traceUse git merge --abort and come back calmly
Waiting weeks to integrateGiant conflictsIntegrate often

Real example: a conflict well resolved

Ana is on feature/new-hours, where she changed the hours to 8:00 to 5:00. Meanwhile, Luis's branch was merged into main, which set Friday until 4:30.

Ana brings main into her branch with git merge main and the conflict appears in contact.md. Instead of accepting her version, Ana checks Luis's commit with git log: "Adjust Friday hours for new schedule". She messages Luis to confirm. Both changes were correct: the general hours changed, and Friday changed too.

Ana combines the two versions, removes the markers, checks that none are left, runs git add, git commit and git push. In the commit message she adds: "Combine new hours with Luis's Friday adjustment". Nobody lost work, and there is a record of how it was resolved.

Practical exercise: cause a conflict on purpose

The best way to lose your fear is to create one yourself, in a safe place.

  1. In practice-git, go to main and update it: git switch main and git pull.

  2. Create a file hours.md with the line Hours: 8:00 to 4:00. Commit and push on main (it is your practice project, here you are allowed to).

  3. Create a branch: git switch -c fix/afternoon-hours. Change the line to Hours: 8:00 to 5:00. Commit.

  4. Go back to main: git switch main. Change the same line to Hours: 7:00 to 4:00. Commit.

  5. Go back to your branch and bring in main: git switch fix/afternoon-hours and git merge main.

  6. Read the conflict warning and run git status.

  7. Open hours.md and identify the three markers and the two blocks.

  8. Try git merge --abort and check with git status that everything is back to normal.

  9. Repeat step 5 and this time resolve it by combining: Hours: 7:00 to 5:00. Remove the markers.

  10. Finish with git add hours.md, git commit and git push -u origin fix/afternoon-hours.

  11. Challenge: repeat the exercise resolving with the VS Code or Cursor buttons, and once more with the Resolve conflicts button of a Merge Request in GitLab.

Chapter checklist

  • I understand that a conflict is not an error: Git is asking me to decide.

  • I know in which situations conflicts appear.

  • I can read the markers <<<<<<<, ======= and >>>>>>>.

  • I know how to cancel with git merge --abort when I need to think.

  • Before resolving, I understand both changes and ask if I'm unsure.

  • I never accept my version by default without reading the other one.

  • I know how to finish the resolution with git add and git commit.

  • I search for forgotten markers and test the result.

  • I know how to resolve conflicts in the editor and in GitLab.

  • I know the practices that prevent large conflicts.

  • I caused and resolved a conflict in my practice project.

PreviousChapter 5. Merge Requests: Ask Permission to IntegrateNextChapter 7. Emergency Kit: Undo Without Losing Work

Chapter 7

Emergency Kit: Undo Without Losing Work

Almost everything in Git can be undone. The key is knowing what you want to undo and whether you already published it. This chapter gives you the four commands to repair mistakes (restore, revert, reset and stash), a safety net for when everything seems lost (reflog), and ready-made recipes for the most common accidents.

Before touching anything: the two questions

When something goes wrong, take a breath and ask yourself:

1. Where is the mistake?

  • In files I changed but haven't committed.

  • In the box (git add), but without a commit.

  • In a commit that only exists on my computer.

  • In a commit I already published with push.

2. Do I want to lose that change or keep it?

With those two answers, you choose the tool. This is the map:

Where the mistake isToolDo I lose the change?
Modified file, without addgit restore fileYes, it is discarded
In the box, without commitgit restore --staged fileNo, it only leaves the box
Local commit, without pushgit resetDepends on the option
Commit already publishedgit revertNo, a commit that cancels it is created
Half-finished work I want to set asidegit stashNo, it is kept aside

And one rule that saves you from many problems: what you already published is not rewritten, it is corrected with a new commit.

git restore: discard changes you haven't saved

You edited a file, made a mess, and want to go back to how it was in the last commit:

git restore contact.md

The file returns to its last saved version.

Warning: this command discards your changes forever. Since you never committed them, Git has no copy. Before using it, check with git diff contact.md what you are about to lose.

To discard the changes of all the files in the folder:

git restore .

Use it with even more care. Check git status first.

To take a file out of the box without losing what you changed (you saw this in chapter 3):

git restore --staged contact.md

This one is harmless: the change stays in your file, it just stops being ready for the commit.

You can also recover a file as it was in an earlier commit:

git restore --source a3f9c21 contact.md

The file is left as it was in that commit. Review it, and if it is useful, make a new commit with that version.

git revert: undo a published commit

You already pushed and discover that a commit has a mistake. Your teammates have already downloaded it. You can't delete it from history without causing them problems. The right thing is to create a new commit that does the opposite:

git log --oneline
git revert a3f9c21

Git creates a commit with a message like Revert "Update opening hours". If the original commit added a line, the revert removes it. If it removed one, the revert puts it back. Then you run git push as usual.

Why it is the safe option:

  • It doesn't erase history. The commit with the mistake is still there, and so is the one that cancelled it. Anyone can see what happened.

  • It doesn't affect your teammates. They just download a new commit, like any other.

  • It can be undone. If you made a mistake while reverting, you can revert the revert.

In GitLab you can also do it with buttons: open the commit or the already merged Merge Request and use the Revert button. GitLab offers to create a Merge Request with the reversal.

git reset: redo commits that only exist on your computer

reset moves your branch backward, as if certain commits had never been made. It is powerful, which is why it has a strict rule: use it only with commits you have not published.

HEAD~1 means "one commit before the current one". HEAD~2, two commits before, and so on.

The three forms of reset

Imagine your last commit was a mistake and you want to undo it. What happens to the changes it had?

OptionThe commitThe changesWhen to use it
--softUndoneThey stay in the box, ready for another commitI want to redo the commit with another message or add something
--mixed (default)UndoneThey stay in your files, outside the boxI want to reorganize what goes into each commit
--hardUndoneDeletedI want to throw all that work away

Examples:

git reset --soft HEAD~1    # Undoes the commit, keeps everything in the box
git reset HEAD~1           # Undoes the commit, keeps the changes in your files
git reset --hard HEAD~1    # Undoes the commit and DELETES its changes

Warning about --hard: it deletes changes from your files without asking for confirmation. Before using it, run git status and git log --oneline and make sure of what you are about to lose. If in doubt, use --soft or --mixed: you can always discard afterward.

And if I already pushed?

If you undo with reset a commit that was already on GitLab, your local branch and the GitLab one become different. Git will reject your push. Forcing it (git push --force) rewrites shared history and can delete your teammates' work. On protected branches, like main, GitLab doesn't even allow it.

For published commits, use revert. The only reasonable exception is your own branch, where nobody else works, and even then it is better to ask the team.

revert, reset and restore: the difference in one sentence

  • restore works with files: it returns them to a saved version.

  • reset works with local commits: it moves your branch backward, as if they had never been made.

  • revert works with published commits: it creates a new commit that cancels another one.

If you only remember one thing: did you already push? Then revert.

git stash: set aside half-finished work

You are in the middle of something, unfinished, and you need to switch branches to handle an emergency. You don't want to commit something incomplete. That is what stash is for: a drawer where you keep your changes temporarily.

git stash push -m "Half-finished form"

Your folder is left clean, as at the last commit. You can switch branches calmly now.

When you come back:

git stash list
stash@{0}: On feature/form: Half-finished form

To recover your changes:

git stash pop

pop takes the changes out of the drawer and applies them to your folder. Other useful options:

CommandWhat it does
git stash applyApplies the changes but keeps them in the drawer too, just in case
git stash dropDeletes the last stash from the drawer
git stash show -pShows which changes are saved
git stash push -u -m "…"Also includes new files that Git was not tracking yet

Three tips:

  • Always give it a message with -m. With three unnamed stashes you won't know which is which.

  • Don't use it as a warehouse. Stash is for minutes or hours, not weeks. If the work is going to last, commit it on a branch.

  • Remember which branch you were on. You can apply a stash on another branch, but usually you want to return to the same one.

git reflog: the safety net

Here is the secret that reassures everyone: Git records every move you make, even the ones that "delete" commits. If you did a reset --hard you regret, or deleted a branch with commits, you can almost always get it back.

git reflog
7b2e104 HEAD@{0}: reset: moving to HEAD~1
a3f9c21 HEAD@{1}: commit: Update opening hours
1c8d3aa HEAD@{2}: checkout: moving from main to feature/hours

Each line is a moment your project went through. To go back to one:

git switch -c rescue a3f9c21

This creates a new branch called rescue exactly at the point you wanted to recover. Check that everything is there and then decide what to do with it.

Two limits you should know: the reflog exists only on your computer, and it keeps the movements for a while (usually several weeks), not forever. And it cannot recover changes that you never saved with a commit.

Recipes for the most common accidents

"I committed to the wrong branch" (without push)

You committed on main when it should have gone on a new branch:

git branch feature/my-change      # Creates the branch pointing at your commit
git reset --hard HEAD~1           # Removes the commit from main
git switch feature/my-change      # Your commit is safe here

If there were two commits, use HEAD~2. Check with git log --oneline before the reset.

"I want to change the message of my last commit" (without push)

git commit --amend -m "Correct message"

"I forgot to add a file to the last commit" (without push)

git add forgotten-file.md
git commit --amend --no-edit

--no-edit keeps the same message.

"I want to undo my last commit but keep the work" (without push)

git reset --soft HEAD~1

"A commit I already published broke something"

git revert commit-code
git push

"I want to throw away all my changes and match GitLab"

git fetch
git status                         # Check what you are about to lose
git reset --hard origin/main       # Your branch becomes identical to GitLab's

If you are on another branch, replace main with its name. Everything that was not published will be lost.

"I deleted a branch that had work"

git reflog                         # Find the last commit of that branch
git switch -c branch-name commit-code

"There are new files I don't know where they came from, and I want to delete them"

git clean -n      # Only shows what it would delete
git clean -f      # Deletes them for real

Always use -n first. git clean deletes files that Git doesn't track, and reflog cannot recover them.

"I uploaded a password or a token"

No command in this list fixes this. Even if you delete it with a new commit, it stays in the history. The first thing to do is change that password or revoke the token immediately, and notify the person responsible for security. We look at it in detail in chapter 10.

Commands that deserve respect

These commands can delete work. They are not forbidden, but before using them stop and check with git status and git log:

CommandWhat it can deleteRecoverable with reflog?
git restore fileUncommitted changesNo
git reset --hardUncommitted changes and local commitsCommits yes; uncommitted changes no
git clean -fFiles Git doesn't trackNo
git branch -DAn unmerged branchYes, for a while
git push --forceOther people's commits on GitLabHard, and it affects the whole team

Real example: the afternoon of three scares

Luis has a complicated afternoon:

  1. He commits on main instead of on his branch. He hasn't pushed. He creates the branch with git branch, removes the commit from main with reset --hard HEAD~1 and keeps working on his branch.

  2. While he works, he is asked to review something urgent on another branch. He saves his half-finished work with git stash push -m "Report adjustments", switches branches, handles the urgent task, comes back and recovers everything with git stash pop.

  3. At the end of the day he discovers that a commit he published in the morning broke the total calculation. Since it is already on GitLab, he doesn't use reset: he runs git revert, pushes, and tells the team.

Three scares, zero work lost, and a history that tells exactly what happened.

Practical exercise: break things on purpose

In your practice-git project, on a new branch called practice/first-aid:

  1. Restore. Modify README.md, look at the change with git diff and discard it with git restore README.md.

  2. Restore --staged. Modify a file, add it with git add and take it out of the box with git restore --staged. Check that the change is still in the file.

  3. Reset --soft. Make a commit with an ugly message. Undo it with git reset --soft HEAD~1 and redo it with a good message.

  4. Stash. Make an uncommitted change, save it with git stash push -m "test", switch to main, come back and recover it with git stash pop.

  5. Revert. Make a commit, publish it with push and then cancel it with git revert. Look at both commits in GitLab.

  6. Reflog. Make a commit, delete it with git reset --hard HEAD~1 and recover it with git reflog and git switch -c rescue commit-code.

  7. Wrong branch. On main of your practice project, make a commit without pushing and move it to a new branch using the recipe from this chapter.

Note on paper which command you used in each case and why. That sheet is your personal first-aid kit.

Chapter checklist

  • Before undoing something, I ask myself where the mistake is and whether I already published it.

  • I know how to discard changes with git restore and that they cannot be recovered.

  • I know how to take a file out of the box with git restore --staged.

  • I use git revert for commits that are already published.

  • I know the three forms of git reset and use --hard with great care.

  • I never run push --force on shared branches.

  • I know how to set work aside with git stash and recover it with git stash pop.

  • I know how to use git reflog to recover "lost" commits.

  • I know how to move a commit made on the wrong branch.

  • I know that an uploaded password must be changed immediately.

  • I did the exercise and I have my own first-aid sheet.

PreviousChapter 6. Conflicts: Don't Erase Someone Else's WorkNextChapter 8. Organize Your Work in GitLab

Chapter 8

Organize Your Work in GitLab

GitLab doesn't only store code: it is also where the team decides what to do, who does it and by when. With issues, labels, milestones, boards and the wiki, all the work stays connected: from the task to the commit that solved it. In this chapter you will learn to use those tools.

The problem with organizing through chat and email

Without a central place, tasks live in loose messages: "did anyone see the form thing?", "I think Luis was going to do it", "was that already fixed?". The questions repeat, tasks get lost and nobody knows what is pending.

With GitLab, each task has its own card, an owner and a status. And every change in the code can be linked to the task that asked for it. Six months later, anyone can see why a change exists and what discussion came before it.

Issues: a card for every task

An issue is a work card. It can be a bug to fix, a new feature, an improvement, a question or a documentation task. You create it in Plan > Issues > New issue.

The parts of an issue

FieldWhat it is for
TitleWhat needs to be done, in one line
DescriptionThe detail: context, steps, criteria for calling it done
AssigneeWho is responsible
LabelsTags to classify: type, priority, area
MilestoneWhich delivery or cycle it belongs to
Due dateDeadline
CommentsThe conversation about the task

Each issue gets an automatic number, such as #42. That number is its identity: you will use it to link it from commits, branches and Merge Requests.

How to write a good issue

A good issue can be picked up by another person without having to ask you anything. To report a bug, use this structure:

## What happens
When sending the contact form without a date, a blank page appears.

## Steps to reproduce
1. Go to the contact page.
2. Fill in name and email, leave the date empty.
3. Press Send.

## What should happen
The message "The date is required" should appear.

## Where
Chrome browser, production version from October 8.

## Screenshots
(attach)

To request something new:

## What is needed
A date filter in the document search.

## Why
Users take a long time to find documents from the current month.

## When we consider it done
- [ ] You can choose a start and end date.
- [ ] If no date is chosen, all results are shown.
- [ ] It works on mobile.

That last section, when we consider it done, is the most valuable. It avoids the argument "I thought it also included…". The - [ ] boxes can be ticked directly in GitLab as you progress.

Templates so you don't start from scratch

If your team always uses the same structure, save it as a template. Create the folder .gitlab/issue_templates/ in the repository and, inside it, one file per template, for example Bug.md and New-feature.md. When you create an issue, GitLab lets you choose it from a menu. The same works for Merge Requests in .gitlab/merge_request_templates/.

Working on an issue

  • Assign it to yourself before starting, so nobody does the same thing in parallel.

  • Comment progress and questions on the issue, not in chat. That way there is a record for whoever comes later.

  • Mention people with @username when you need their opinion.

  • Close it when it is finished, or let it close on its own with the Merge Request (we see this below).

Labels: classify to find

Labels are colored tags you put on issues and Merge Requests to classify and filter them. They are created in Manage > Labels.

A simple set that works in many teams:

GroupLabels
Typebug, new feature, improvement, documentation
Priorityhigh priority, medium priority, low priority
Statusto do, in progress, in review, blocked
Areaform, reports, database, server

Tips:

  • Few and clear. With a hundred labels nobody knows which to use.

  • One color per group. For example, reds for priority and blues for type. They can be read at a glance.

  • Write a description for each label, so everyone uses it the same way.

  • Agree on them as a team. Labels only work if everyone applies them with the same criteria.

With labels you can filter the issue list and answer questions like "which high-priority bugs are still open?" in two clicks.

Connecting commits, branches and Merge Requests to an issue

Here is the magic: GitLab links everything automatically if you mention the issue number.

In the branch name. Include the number, as you saw in chapter 4: fix/42-empty-date-form.

In the commit message. Add the reference:

git commit -m "Validate required date in contact form (#42)"

The commit will appear mentioned inside issue 42.

In the Merge Request description. Use a closing keyword:

Closes #42

When the Merge Request is merged into the main branch, GitLab closes issue 42 automatically. Fixes #42 and Resolves #42 work too. If you only want to link it without closing it, write Related to #42 or simply #42.

The most convenient shortcut: create the branch from the issue. Inside an issue you will see the Create merge request button (or Create branch). GitLab creates a branch with the issue's number and title, and a draft Merge Request already linked. You only need to bring it to your computer:

git fetch
git switch 42-empty-date-form

The result is a complete, traceable chain: issue → branch → commits → Merge Request → closed issue.

Milestones: group work by deliveries

A milestone is a goal with a date: a version, a two-week cycle or a delivery to a client. It groups the issues and Merge Requests that must be ready by that date. It is created in Plan > Milestones.

Examples:

  • Version 1.5 — from November 1 to 15.

  • Sprint 23 — two weeks of work.

  • Reports module delivery — for the date agreed with the user department.

When you open a milestone, GitLab shows a progress bar: how many issues are closed and how many are left. It is the fastest way to answer "are we going to make the date?".

Tip: if a milestone has too many open issues close to its date, it is better to consciously move some to the next one than to pretend everything will get done.

Boards: see the work at a glance

A board shows issues as cards in columns, in Kanban style. You find it in Plan > Issue boards.

By default it has two columns, Open and Closed. You can add columns based on labels. A typical board:

To doIn progressIn reviewClosed
#45 Date filter#42 Empty date in form#40 Phone in footer#38 New logo
#47 Export to Excel

When you drag a card from one column to another, GitLab changes its label automatically. If you drag it to Closed, the issue closes.

How to use it well:

  • Review it together in the team meeting, instead of asking one by one "how is it going?".

  • Limit what is "in progress". If one person has six cards in that column, something isn't working.

  • Keep it up to date. An outdated board is worse than no board, because it misleads.

  • Filter. You can view the board of a single milestone, a single person or a single label.

The wiki: the project's memory

The wiki is a documentation space inside the project, with pages written in Markdown (chapter 13). It is under Plan > Wiki.

What usually goes in the wiki:

  • User guides for the people who use the product.

  • Team procedures: how to publish a version, how to handle an emergency.

  • Important decisions and their reasons.

  • The project glossary and contacts.

  • Minutes or summaries of technical meetings.

Creating a page is simple: New page, write the title, the content in Markdown and save. To link another wiki page, use [text](page-name). Pages with names like guides/installation are organized into folders.

A useful fact: inside, the wiki is another Git repository. It has a change history, and you can clone it and edit it on your computer. The address to clone it appears in the wiki itself.

Wiki or a docs/ folder in the repository?

Wikidocs/ folder
How it is editedFrom the browser, easyWith commits and Merge Requests
Review before publishingNoYes, like the code
Goes along with each version of the codeNoYes
Ideal forGeneral guides, processes, team notesTechnical instructions that change with the code

Many teams use both: the wiki for general matters and docs/ for what must stay in sync with each version of the system.

Note about GitLab versions. Some advanced organization features (such as multiple boards per project, issue weights or epics) depend on the GitLab plan your organization uses. Everything in this chapter works on the free version, although the menus may vary a little.

On GitHub it is called… Issues and labels exist in the same way. Milestones too. Boards are called Projects. The wiki is in the Wiki tab. To close issues from a Pull Request you use the same words: Closes #42.

Real example: from report to closure

A user reports by email that the form shows a blank page. Ana doesn't leave it in the email: she creates the issue #42 Blank page when sending form without a date, with the steps to reproduce it, the labels bug and high priority, and assigns it to the milestone Version 1.5.

Luis assigns it to himself and moves it to In progress on the board. From the issue he presses Create merge request, brings the branch to his computer, fixes it and commits with (#42) in the message. The Merge Request already says Closes #42.

Ana reviews, approves, and Luis merges. The issue closes on its own and the card moves to Closed. The next day, Ana replies to the user with the link to the issue. If someone asks in six months why that validation exists, everything is there: the report, the discussion, the change and the review.

Practical exercise

In your practice-git project:

  1. Create four labels: bug, improvement, documentation and in progress, each with a color and a description.

  2. Create a milestone called Practice week 1 with a date seven days from now.

  3. Create three issues: an invented bug (using the bug template), an improvement (with done criteria as checkboxes) and a documentation task. Assign them to the milestone and add labels.

  4. Open the board and add the in progress column. Move an issue to that column and check that its label changed.

  5. From the documentation issue, use Create merge request. Bring the branch to your computer, make a change to the README with a commit that mentions the issue, and publish it.

  6. Check that the Merge Request description has Closes #number. Merge it and verify that the issue closed on its own and the milestone advanced.

  7. Create a wiki page called How we work with your branch naming convention and commit message convention.

  8. Challenge: create the folder .gitlab/issue_templates/ with a template Bug.md and use it to create a new issue.

Chapter checklist

  • I can create an issue with a clear title, description and done criteria.

  • I use templates to report bugs and request new features.

  • I assign the issue to myself before starting and comment progress on it.

  • I know how to create and use labels with an agreed criterion.

  • I link issues from branches, commits and Merge Requests.

  • I use Closes #number to close issues automatically.

  • I know how to create a branch and a Merge Request directly from an issue.

  • I know how to create a milestone and read its progress.

  • I know how to use the board, and I understand that moving cards changes labels.

  • I know how to create wiki pages and when to use docs/ instead.

PreviousChapter 7. Emergency Kit: Undo Without Losing WorkNextChapter 9. CI/CD: Learn to Read the Traffic Light

Chapter 9

CI/CD: Learn to Read the Traffic Light

CI/CD is a set of automatic checks and tasks that GitLab runs every time you publish changes. Its result is a traffic light: green if everything passed, red if something failed. In this book we will not configure or create pipelines. The goal of this chapter is for you to understand what they are, know how to read their result, and know what to do when something turns red.

Important clarification. Configuring CI/CD is a full topic on its own and is usually done by someone with infrastructure experience. Here you will learn to recognize it and read it, which is what you need to work on a team that already uses it. You will not modify pipeline configuration files in any exercise.

What CI/CD means

They are two related ideas:

CI: continuous integration. Every time someone publishes changes, an automatic system checks that the project still works: that it can be built, that the tests pass, that the code follows the team's rules. The idea is to detect errors in minutes, not days later.

CD: continuous delivery or deployment. When changes pass the checks, the system automatically prepares a version ready to publish (continuous delivery) or even publishes it to the server (continuous deployment).

What it doesWho decides to publish
Continuous integrationChecks every change automaticallyNobody publishes yet
Continuous deliveryLeaves a version ready to publishA person presses the button
Continuous deploymentPublishes automaticallyNobody: if everything passes, it is published by itself

A comparison: quality control

Think of a bread factory. Before a tray leaves for the store, it goes through several stations: the dough is checked, it is baked, the temperature is measured, it is packed and labeled. If one station detects a problem, the tray does not move on.

A pipeline does the same with your code. Each station is an automatic check. If one fails, it stops and warns you. That way errors stay in the factory and don't reach the users.

What pipelines are for

A pipeline is the list of automatic tasks that run with your changes. The most common tasks:

  • Building the project, to check that nothing was left broken.

  • Running automatic tests, which verify that what worked still works.

  • Checking the style of the code, according to the team's rules.

  • Looking for security problems, such as passwords left in the code or libraries with known flaws.

  • Publishing to a test or production server.

The benefit for you: you don't have to remember to check everything by hand before each Merge Request. And the team has a minimum guarantee that what enters main works.

How a pipeline is built

A pipeline is organized into stages, and each stage has one or more jobs:

Stage: build   →   Stage: test              →   Stage: publish
  [compile]          [unit tests]                 [deploy to test]
                     [style check]

  • Stages run in order: if "test" fails, "publish" does not run.

  • The jobs of the same stage usually run at the same time.

The jobs are run by machines called runners. You don't need to know more about them for now: just that they are the "stations" where the work is done.

Where the configuration lives

All of this is defined in a file called .gitlab-ci.yml, at the root of the repository. If a project has that file, it has pipelines.

Just so you recognize it if you see it, a very simple example:

stages:
  - test

tests:
  stage: test
  script:
    - echo "Running tests"

You don't need to understand or modify it. If one of your Merge Requests changes that file by accident, let the person responsible know before merging.

Where to see the result

GitLab shows you the pipeline status in several places:

  1. In the Merge Request. Below the description there is a box with the pipeline status. It is where you will look at it most.

  2. In the commit list. Each commit shows a small icon with its status.

  3. In the Build > Pipelines menu. The complete list of all the project's pipelines, with date, branch, author and duration.

  4. On the project's main page, next to the last commit.

When you click a pipeline, you will see a diagram with its stages and jobs, each with its own status icon.

How to tell whether a pipeline passed or failed

Icon and statusMeaningWhat to do
Green, passedEverything passedYou can continue with the review and the merge
Red, failedAt least one job failedDon't merge. Check what failed
Blue, runningIt is runningWait
Gray, pending / createdWaiting for a free runnerWait. If it takes long, let someone know
Orange, warningA job marked as "allowed to fail" failedReview it, although it does not block
Crossed-out gray, canceledSomeone canceled itRun it again if needed
skippedIt did not run because an earlier stage failedFix the stage that failed
manual (gear or play icon)Waits for a person to start itOnly the person with that responsibility starts it

If the project is set up that way, GitLab won't let you merge a Merge Request with a red pipeline. It is a protection, not an obstacle.

What to do when a pipeline fails

A red pipeline is not a scolding: it is information. This is the process:

1. Identify which job failed. Open the pipeline and look for the job with the red X.

2. Read the log. Click the job to see its log: everything the machine wrote while working. The error is almost always at the end. Scroll to the last lines and look for words like Error, FAILED or failed, usually in red.

3. Relate it to your change. Ask yourself: what did I change that could have caused this? Often the message mentions a specific file or test.

4. Fix it on your branch. Make the fix, commit and push. The pipeline runs again automatically.

5. If it has nothing to do with your change, it may be a passing fault (for example, a network problem on the runner). You can retry the job with the Retry button. If it fails the same way again, it is not a passing fault.

6. If you don't understand the error, ask. Write in the Merge Request with the link to the failed job and mention the person responsible for the pipeline. Copying the last lines of the log into your message helps a lot.

What you should never do

  • Merge with the pipeline red "because it's surely nothing".

  • Delete or disable a test so the pipeline passes.

  • Modify .gitlab-ci.yml to skip a check.

  • Press Retry many times, hoping it passes by luck.

All these actions hide the problem instead of solving it, and the problem ends up reaching production.

What a green pipeline does and doesn't mean

A green pipeline means that the checks that exist passed. It does not mean the change is perfect. If the project has no tests for a certain part, an error there will not be detected by anyone.

That is why the pipeline does not replace the human review from chapter 5. They are complementary: the machine checks what is repetitive and measurable; people check the logic, the clarity and whether the change really solves what was requested.

On GitHub it is called… GitHub Actions. The configuration lives in the folder .github/workflows/. The traffic light works the same way: a green check or a red X next to each commit and in each Pull Request.

Real example: the red that saved a release

Luis opens a Merge Request that changes the date format in a report. A few minutes later, the pipeline turns red.

He opens the tests job and scrolls to the end of the log. He sees that a test called "monthly report shows correct totals" failed: the format change broke the way the report grouped months. Luis wouldn't have noticed by testing by hand, because he only checked the daily report screen.

He fixes it, pushes, and the pipeline turns green. Ana reviews the logic and approves. The error never reached the people who use the report.

Practical exercise

Your practice project has no pipelines, and in this book we won't configure them. To practice reading them, use a public project:

  1. Find an active open source project on GitLab.com and go to Build > Pipelines.

  2. Find one green pipeline and one red one. Note their branch, author and duration.

  3. Open the red pipeline and identify which stage and which job failed.

  4. Open the log of the failed job, scroll to the end and copy the line that explains the error. Try to describe in your own words what happened.

  5. Check whether the later stages ended up as skipped.

  6. Open a recent Merge Request of the project and find the pipeline box.

  7. Look for the .gitlab-ci.yml file at the root of the repository and count how many stages it has. Just look at it.

  8. Write a practice message (without publishing it) as if you were asking for help with a failed pipeline: which job failed, the link and the error lines.

Chapter checklist

  • I can explain what continuous integration is and what continuous delivery or deployment is.

  • I know what a pipeline, a stage and a job are.

  • I know that the configuration lives in .gitlab-ci.yml and that I must not modify it without the person responsible.

  • I find the pipeline status in the Merge Request, in the commits and in Build > Pipelines.

  • I recognize the statuses: passed, failed, running, pending, skipped, canceled and manual.

  • I know how to read the log of a failed job starting from the end.

  • I know what to do when it fails: fix it, retry if it is a passing fault, or ask for help.

  • I never merge on red and never disable tests to get past them.

  • I understand that a green pipeline does not replace human review.

  • I understand that this book does not configure pipelines.

PreviousChapter 8. Organize Your Work in GitLabNextChapter 10. Security and Good Practices

Chapter 10

Security and Good Practices

A repository stores much more than code: it stores the work of a whole team and, sometimes, the entry door to important systems. This chapter covers the five protections every team should have: well-granted permissions, protected branches, secrets kept out of the repository, secure accounts, and a review before merging or publishing.

Why security starts in Git

Three real situations that repeat in many organizations:

  • Someone accidentally uploads a file with the database password. The repository is internal, but dozens of people can see it, and any cloned copy keeps it.

  • A person who no longer works at the organization still has access to the project months later.

  • An unreviewed change is merged straight into main and published, with a bug that takes the service down for users.

None of them requires a sophisticated attack. All of them are avoided with basic configuration and good habits.

Roles and permissions

In GitLab, each person who takes part in a project has a role. The role decides what they can see and what they can do. They are managed in Manage > Members.

RoleWhat it can do, in shortFor whom
GuestSee the project (depending on its visibility) and create issuesUsers who report bugs
ReporterRead the code, comment, manage issuesAnalysts, managers who follow progress
DeveloperCreate branches, push, open Merge RequestsThe team that develops
MaintainerMerge on protected branches, configure the projectTechnical leads, project owners
OwnerFull control, including deleting the projectVery few people

The exact names and details may vary depending on your organization's GitLab version, which may even have an additional role. The idea is the same everywhere.

The principle of least privilege
