Each person should have the minimum role they need for their work, and nothing more. Not because you distrust anyone, but because it reduces the possible damage from a mistake or a stolen account.

Good practices with permissions:

  • Don't give Maintainer "just in case". Start with Developer and raise the role when needed.

  • Use expiration dates for temporary access: consultants, interns, one-off support. GitLab lets you set a date on which access is removed automatically.

  • Review the member list periodically, for example every three months. Remove anyone who no longer participates.

  • Remove access the same day someone leaves the organization or the project.

  • Use groups when several projects share the same team. That way you manage permissions in one place.

  • Never share accounts. Each person uses their own. Otherwise the history loses its meaning: you don't know who did what.

Protected branches

A protected branch has special rules about who can change it and how. It is configured in Settings > Repository > Protected branches. In most projects, main comes protected by default.

What you can define:

OptionWhat it controlsRecommended setting for main
Allowed to mergeWho can merge Merge RequestsMaintainers
Allowed to push and mergeWho can push directlyNobody (No one)
Allowed to force pushWhether history can be rewrittenDisabled

With that setting, the only way to change main is with a Merge Request that someone with permission merges. It is exactly the flow from chapters 4 and 5, but now it is mandatory, not just good intention.

Other protections worth knowing:

  • Required approvals. Requiring one or more people to approve before merging. Depending on your organization's GitLab plan, it may be mandatory or just informative.

  • Successful pipeline to merge. In Settings > Merge requests, the option Pipelines must succeed prevents merging on red (chapter 9).

  • All threads resolved. The option All threads must be resolved prevents merging with pending comments.

  • Protected tags. Just like branches, you can protect version tags (v*) so that only certain people can create them (chapter 13).

If you try to push directly to a protected branch, you will see an error like You are not allowed to push code to protected branches. It is not a failure: it is the protection working. Create a branch and open a Merge Request.

Never store secrets in the repository

A secret is any piece of data that gives access to something:

  • Passwords for databases, servers or services.

  • API tokens and keys.

  • Private SSH keys or certificates.

  • Email or cloud service credentials.

  • Connection strings that include a username and password.

Why it is so serious

Remember that Git stores the whole history. If you upload a password and in the next commit you delete it, the password is still there, in the previous commit. Anyone who has a copy of the repository can find it with git log -S (chapter 12).

Also:

  • Every person who cloned the project has a copy of that history.

  • Forks have it too.

  • In public repositories there are automated programs that constantly search the internet for exposed secrets. A published key can be used by third parties in very little time.

How to keep them out

1. Use environment variables and an ignored .env file. Secrets go in a local file, for example .env, which never enters the repository:

# .env  (NOT uploaded)
DB_PASSWORD=the-real-password
API_TOKEN=the-real-token

And in .gitignore:

.env
*.pem
*.key

2. Upload a template without real values. Create a .env.example with the variable names and example values, so the team knows what to configure:

# .env.example  (YES, uploaded)
DB_PASSWORD=write-the-password-here
API_TOKEN=write-the-token-here

3. For pipelines, use GitLab variables. The secrets the CI/CD needs are stored in Settings > CI/CD > Variables, marked as Masked (hidden in logs) and Protected (only available on protected branches). Usually the person responsible for the pipelines configures them.

4. Review before every commit. Here the rule from chapter 3 returns: look before you save. A git status and a git diff --staged catch most accidents. Be wary of any file you didn't expect to see.

5. Turn on automatic detection if your team can. There are tools that check every commit for secrets before they enter: GitLab's secret detection analysis in pipelines, or programs that run on your computer before each commit (called pre-commit hooks). Ask whether your team uses one.

If you uploaded a secret: what to do, in this order

  1. Change or revoke the secret immediately. Change the password, revoke the token, generate a new key. This step is the only one that really protects you. Do it even if the repository is private, and even if you think nobody saw it.

  2. Notify the person responsible for security or your technical manager. No shame: it is a common accident, and reporting it quickly is the professional thing to do.

  3. Check whether the secret was used, with help from whoever manages that system (access logs, unusual activity).

  4. Remove the secret from the code with a new commit and move the value to .env or to GitLab variables.

  5. Cleaning up the history (deleting the secret from all old commits) is possible with specialized tools, but it rewrites the history of the whole team. Do it only with support from someone experienced, and remember that it does not replace step 1.

Protect your account

Your GitLab account is the key to every project you take part in:

  • Turn on two-step verification (2FA) in your profile, under Preferences > Account. If someone obtains your password, they still won't be able to get in.

  • Tokens with expiration and minimum permissions (chapter 2). Delete the ones you no longer use.

  • Put a passphrase on your SSH key and never share the private key.

  • Lock your computer when you step away. An open session is an open account.

  • Be wary of emails asking for your credentials, even if they look like they come from GitLab. Always log in by typing the address yourself.

What to check before merging

Whether you are merging your own Merge Request or someone else's:

  • The source branch and the target branch are the right ones.

  • The description explains what changes and why.

  • I reviewed all the files in the Changes tab.

  • There are no secrets, .env files, keys or personal data.

  • There are no files unrelated to the task, temporary or test files.

  • There are no forgotten <<<<<<< conflict markers.

  • The pipeline is green.

  • All comments are addressed and the threads resolved.

  • It has the required approvals.

  • It does not modify .gitlab-ci.yml or sensitive settings without the knowledge of the person responsible.

What to check before deploying

Publishing to production is the moment of greatest risk. Even if a designated person usually does it, it helps that the whole team knows the list:

  • Everything to be published is merged into the right branch and has a green pipeline.

  • There is a version tag and its notes (chapter 13).

  • It is known exactly which changes go in, by comparing with the previous version (chapter 12).

  • There is a plan to roll back if something fails: which version to restore and how.

  • If there are database changes, a recent backup exists.

  • A low-usage time was chosen and users are warned if there will be an interruption.

  • Someone is available to check that everything works after publishing.

  • Nothing is published on a Friday afternoon, except in emergencies. If something fails, nobody wants to fix it over the weekend.

Good practices for teamwork

Technical security is of little use if the team has no clear agreements. These practices summarize the book:

  1. Nobody works directly on main. Branches for everything.

  2. Short branches. Days, not weeks.

  3. Small commits, with clear messages. One commit, one idea.

  4. git pull when starting the day and before creating a branch.

  5. Every change goes through a Merge Request and review. Including urgent ones.

  6. Every task has its issue. Important conversations happen there, not in chat.

  7. Look before you save and before you merge.

  8. Comments about the code, not about the person.

  9. Conflicts: understand both sides. Never delete the other person's work.

  10. What is published is not rewritten. It is corrected with a new commit.

  11. Secrets never enter the repository. And if they do, they are changed immediately.

  12. Document your agreements in the wiki: conventions for branches, commits and labels, and who to ask.

This last one is key. A new team doesn't need to guess its rules if they are written down.

Real example: the password uploaded on a Monday

Luis is setting up the connection to the test database and, in a hurry, commits the config.py file with the password written in it. He pushes to his branch and opens a Merge Request.

Ana reviews the Changes tab and sees the password. She doesn't approve. She messages Luis privately and he acts in order: he asks the database administrator to change the password immediately, notifies the technical management, moves the configuration to environment variables with an ignored .env and a .env.example, and makes a new commit.

Since the branch only existed in the internal project and the password had already been changed, the team decides with management how to clean up that branch's history. The incident was resolved within an hour. The next day, the team adds automatic secret detection to its improvement list and the .env rule to the wiki.

Practical exercise

In your practice-git project (you are Owner, so you can configure everything):

  1. Go to Manage > Members and review the roles. If you practice with someone, give them the Developer role with an expiration date.

  2. In Settings > Repository > Protected branches, configure main: merge only by Maintainers, direct push No one, force push disabled.

  3. Try to push directly to main from your computer and read the error. Then make the change through a branch and a Merge Request.

  4. In Settings > Merge requests, enable All threads must be resolved.

  5. Create a .env file with an invented value, for example TEST_KEY=12345. Check that your .gitignore excludes it with git status. If it appears, add .env to .gitignore.

  6. Create and upload a .env.example with the same variable and an example value.

  7. Turn on two-step verification in your GitLab account.

  8. Review your access tokens and delete the ones you don't use.

  9. Write a wiki page Team rules with your 12 good practices, adapted to your way of working.

Chapter checklist

  • I know the GitLab roles and apply the principle of least privilege.

  • I know how to use expiration dates and periodically review members.

  • I know how to protect main: no direct push and no force push.

  • I know the options for successful pipeline and resolved threads before merging.

  • I know what a secret is and why deleting it in a new commit is not enough.

  • I use an ignored .env and a .env.example for configuration.

  • I know what to do if I upload a secret: change it first, report it after.

  • I have two-step verification turned on.

  • I use the review checklist before merging.

  • I know the review checklist before deploying.

  • My team has its rules written down in the wiki.

PreviousChapter 9. CI/CD: Learn to Read the Traffic LightNextChapter 11. Git with an Agent: You Decide, the AI Executes

Chapter 11

Git with an Agent: You Decide, the AI Executes

An AI agent or a script can do the repetitive parts of Git for you: pulling changes, preparing commits, publishing them and opening Merge Requests. Used well, it saves you time and mistakes. Used badly, it can publish something nobody reviewed. The rule of this chapter is the one in the title: automation executes, but you make the decisions.

My workflow

I work this way every day: I use Claude to plan the change, Cursor to run it on the code, and I review every commit before I push. The AI proposes and writes. I read, understand, correct and decide what gets published.

That last step is not optional. An agent can write code that looks right and isn't, touch files you didn't ask for, or solve a problem in a way that doesn't fit the rest of the project. If you publish without looking, the person responsible for the change is still you, not the tool.

That is why this chapter is at the end of the book, and not at the beginning. To supervise an agent you need to understand everything before: what each command does, how to read a diff, when there is a conflict and how to undo a mistake.

What can be automated and what cannot

TaskCan it be automated?Condition
Checking which branch and state you are inYesIt should always be done before any action
git pull to bring in changesYesStops if a conflict appears
Creating a branch with the right nameYesFollowing the team's convention
Proposing the commit messageYesYou read it and approve or correct it
Making the commitYesAfter you see the diff and confirm
git push to your branchYesAfter your confirmation, never to main
Opening a Merge Request with a descriptionYesYou review the text before asking for review
Resolving conflictsNoRequires understanding both sides (chapter 6)
Merging into a protected branchNoA person decides, after review
Approving a Merge RequestNoReview is human (chapter 5)
push --force, reset --hard, deleting branchesNoActions that can destroy work
Handling secretsNoPasswords are never handed to an agent

The five rules of safe automation

1. Validate before acting

Before any command, the automation checks:

  • That it is inside a Git repository.

  • Which branch it is on, and that it is not main or another protected branch.

  • That there is no half-done merge or conflict.

  • Which files changed, and whether any look suspicious (.env, keys, huge files).

If something doesn't add up, it stops and warns you. It never "fixes it" on its own.

2. Preview and confirmation

Before committing, pushing or opening a Merge Request, the automation shows you exactly what it is going to do:

  • The list of files and the diff.

  • The proposed commit message.

  • The branch it is going to publish to.

And it waits for you to confirm explicitly. A "yes" typed by you, not a "continue automatically".

3. Mandatory human review at critical points

There are three moments when the automation always stops and hands control back to you:

  • Conflicts. It tells you which files clash, and you resolve them.

  • Protected branches. It never pushes or merges to them.

  • Merge Requests. It can open them, but review, approval and merging belong to people.

4. Log of every command and result

Every action is recorded in a log file: date, project, branch, command and result. That way you can audit what the automation did and, if something went wrong, know exactly what to revert (chapter 7). A log like this one:

2026-10-08 10:42:11 | practice-git | git pull (branch feature/about-me) | ok
2026-10-08 10:43:02 | practice-git | commit a3f9c21: Add about me section | ok
2026-10-08 10:43:05 | practice-git | push to origin/feature/about-me | ok

With the commit code (a3f9c21) you can run git show to review it or git revert to cancel it.

5. Least privilege

The automation uses your credentials with just the right permissions: it can write to branches, but not merge into main (that is what the protected branches of chapter 10 are for). If you use a token for it, give it an expiration date and only the permissions it needs.

Option A: a simple script with confirmation

A script is a list of commands saved in a file that runs in one go. It isn't clever: it always does the same thing. That is exactly why it is predictable.

This script applies the five rules. It takes what you put in the box with git add, shows it to you, looks for sensitive files, asks for the message and the confirmation, commits and pushes to your branch, and logs everything:

#!/usr/bin/env bash
# publish.sh: saves and publishes what is in the box, with human review
set -euo pipefail

LOG="$HOME/.git-assistant.log"
log() { echo "$(date '+%Y-%m-%d %H:%M:%S') | $(basename "$PWD") | $*" | tee -a "$LOG"; }

# 1. Validate: repository, branch and state
if ! git rev-parse --is-inside-work-tree >/dev/null 2>&1; then
  echo "You are not inside a Git repository."; exit 1
fi

BRANCH=$(git branch --show-current)
if [[ -z "$BRANCH" || "$BRANCH" == "main" || "$BRANCH" == "master" ]]; then
  echo "You are on '$BRANCH'. Create a branch before publishing."; exit 1
fi

if [[ -f "$(git rev-parse --git-dir)/MERGE_HEAD" ]]; then
  echo "There is a half-done merge. Resolve it first (chapter 6)."; exit 1
fi

# 2. Bring in changes from GitLab if the branch is already published
if git rev-parse --abbrev-ref '@{u}' >/dev/null 2>&1; then
  if git pull --no-rebase; then
    log "git pull (branch $BRANCH) | ok"
  else
    log "git pull (branch $BRANCH) | conflict or error, stopping"
    echo "There are conflicts or errors. Resolve them yourself and run it again."; exit 1
  fi
fi

# 3. Preview of what is going to be saved
if git diff --cached --quiet; then
  echo "Nothing is in the box. Use git add with the files you want to save."; exit 1
fi
echo "===== Files to be saved ====="
git --no-pager diff --cached --stat
echo "===== Changes ====="
git --no-pager diff --cached

# 4. Alert for sensitive files
if git diff --cached --name-only | grep -Eiq '(^|/)\.env$|\.pem$|\.key$|id_rsa|id_ed25519'; then
  log "ALERT: sensitive file in the box, stopping"
  echo "Some files look like secrets. Take them out of the box (chapter 10)."; exit 1
fi

# 5. Message and human confirmation
read -r -p "Commit message: " MSG
if [[ -z "$MSG" ]]; then echo "The message cannot be empty."; exit 1; fi

read -r -p "Save and publish to '$BRANCH'? Type yes to confirm: " OK
if [[ "$OK" != "yes" ]]; then log "cancelled by the person"; echo "Cancelled."; exit 0; fi

git commit -m "$MSG"
log "commit $(git rev-parse --short HEAD): $MSG | ok"

git push -u origin "$BRANCH"
log "push to origin/$BRANCH | ok"

echo "Done. Open or review the Merge Request in GitLab. A person decides the merge."

How to use it:

  1. Save the text in a file publish.sh, in a folder outside your projects (for example, ~/scripts/).

  2. Give it execution permission once: chmod +x ~/scripts/publish.sh.

  3. In your project, look and choose your changes as usual: git status, git diff, git add file.

  4. Run ~/scripts/publish.sh and follow the questions.

  5. Review the log whenever you like: cat ~/.git-assistant.log.

Notice what the script does not do: it doesn't pick files for you, doesn't merge, doesn't resolve conflicts, doesn't force anything and doesn't touch main. Those decisions are still yours. On Windows, run it from Git Bash.

Option B: an AI agent with clear rules

An AI agent, like the ones built into editors such as Cursor or into coding assistants, is more flexible than a script: it understands instructions in natural language ("add a date filter and push it to a branch"), writes code and runs commands. That flexibility is exactly what demands more control.

Give it written rules

Most agents let you leave permanent instructions in a file in the project (depending on the tool, it may be called AGENTS.md, an editor rules file or another name its documentation indicates). The agent reads those instructions before working. An example you can adapt:

# Rules for working with Git in this project

## Before acting
- Run git status and tell me which branch you are on and what changed.
- Never work on main. If you are on main, create a branch with the format
  type/number-description (for example fix/42-empty-date) and ask me to confirm.
- Run git pull before starting.

## Commits
- One commit per idea. Message in English, starting with a verb.
- Before each commit, show me the diff and the proposed message, and wait for my "yes".
- Never add .env files, keys, credentials or files outside the task.

## Publishing
- Only push to the working branch, never to main or protected branches.
- Before pushing, ask me for confirmation.
- You can draft the Merge Request description, but do not merge it.

## Forbidden without my explicit authorization
- git push --force, git reset --hard, git clean, deleting branches.
- Resolving conflicts: if one appears, stop and tell me which files clash.
- Modifying .gitlab-ci.yml or the project configuration.

## Log
- When you finish, give me the list of commands you ran and their results.

How to ask for things

A good request to the agent describes what you want and what the limits are:

"Add required date validation to the contact form, for issue #42. Work on a new branch. When you finish, show me the diff and the commit message before saving. Don't push without my confirmation."

Compare that with "fix the form and push it". The first makes the scope, the branch and the checkpoints clear. The second gives the agent too much freedom.

Review what the agent did

When the agent shows you the diff, read it as if a new colleague wrote it on their first day (chapter 5):

  • Did it do only what you asked, or did it touch other files?

  • Do you understand every line that changed? If you don't, don't approve it: ask it to explain.

  • Does it work? Test it yourself, don't rely on "the agent says it works".

  • Does the commit message describe the change well?

  • Is there anything that looks like a secret, personal data or a temporary file?

If something doesn't convince you, ask for the change or make it yourself. Approving without understanding is exactly the "save everything without looking" mistake of chapter 3, only faster.

What you must understand even when it is automated

Automation doesn't free you from knowing Git. It frees you from typing the commands, not from understanding them. Before using an agent or a script, you should be able to answer:

  • What is the difference between commit and push? (chapter 1)

  • How do I read a diff and what changed? (chapter 3)

  • Which branch am I on, and why must I not be on main? (chapter 4)

  • What happens in a Merge Request and who decides the merge? (chapter 5)

  • What is a conflict and how is it resolved without losing work? (chapter 6)

  • How do I undo a published commit? (chapter 7)

  • Why does a secret never enter the repository? (chapter 10)

If the automation fails halfway through, and one day it will, you are the one who has to understand what state the repository was left in and how to continue.

Common mistakes when automating

MistakeConsequenceHow to avoid it
Giving the agent permission for everything without confirmationPublishes changes without reviewWritten rules and confirmation before commit and push
Automatic git add .Sensitive or unrelated files get inThe person chooses the files, or a sensitive-file alert
Letting the agent resolve conflictsOther people's work is silently lostRule: on a conflict, it stops
Not keeping a logYou don't know what it did or how to revertA log of every command with the commit code
Approving diffs you don't understandErrors that are hard to traceAsk for an explanation or don't approve
Pasting passwords to the agent in chatThe secret ends up in places you don't controlSecrets go in .env or in GitLab variables

Real example: one change from start to finish with an agent

Ana receives issue #45 Add date filter to search. She asks the agent: "Work on issue #45 on a new branch. Show me the plan before touching code."

The agent checks the state: Ana is on main, up to date and with no changes. It proposes creating feature/45-date-filter and Ana confirms. Then it proposes a three-step plan; Ana asks it to remove one step that wasn't needed. The agent writes the code and shows her the diff: it touched the search file and, without being asked, a styles file. Ana asks it to revert that second change.

With a clean diff, Ana tests the filter on her computer and it works. The agent proposes the message Add date filter to search (#45). Ana approves it, confirms the push, and the agent prepares the Merge Request description with Closes #45. Ana reviews it, adjusts it and assigns Luis as reviewer.

Luis reviews and approves. Ana merges. The agent saved time writing code and commands; every decision was Ana's.

Practical exercise

In your practice-git project:

  1. Create the rules file for your agent (or the one your editor uses) with the example from this chapter, adapted to how you work. Publish it with a normal Merge Request.

  2. Create an issue: "Add a hobbies section to the about-me.md file".

  3. Ask your agent to work on that issue following the rules: new branch, diff and message before the commit, confirmation before the push.

  4. When it shows you the diff, review it with this chapter's list. Ask for at least one correction, even a small one.

  5. Confirm the commit and the push. Ask it for the list of commands it ran.

  6. Verify manually in GitLab: that the branch exists with the right name, that the commit has a good message, that the diff in the Merge Request is the one you approved, and that the description includes Closes #number.

  7. Also verify from the terminal with git log --oneline and git show.

  8. Merge the Merge Request yourself and check that the issue closed.

  9. If you prefer the script, repeat the steps with publish.sh and check the log file at the end.

Chapter checklist

  • I understand that automation executes, but the decisions are mine.

  • I know which tasks can be automated and which never should be.

  • My automation validates repository, branch and state before acting.

  • I see a preview and confirm before every commit and push.

  • Conflicts, protected branches and merges are always handled by a person.

  • Every command is logged so I can audit or revert it.

  • I have written rules for my agent.

  • I know how to ask the agent for tasks with clear scope and limits.

  • I review the agent's diff and don't approve what I don't understand.

  • I never give secrets to the agent.

  • I manually verified in GitLab a change made with the agent's help.

PreviousChapter 10. Security and Good PracticesNextChapter 12. Investigating History: Git as a Detective

Chapter 12

Investigating History: Git as a Detective

Git's history is not just a record: it is the tool for answering questions like "who changed this?", "when did it start failing?" or "what is the difference between my branch and main?". In this chapter you will learn to search the history, compare versions and understand how branches are combined inside.

Why it is worth learning to investigate

When something fails in a system, the first question is almost never "how do I fix it?". It is "what changed?". If you know how to search the history, you find the responsible commit, who made it and why in minutes. Without that, you have to guess.

It is also useful for calmer things: understanding why a part of the project is written a certain way, remembering what you did last week, or preparing a summary of changes for your manager.

This is where the value of the good commit messages from chapter 3 shows. A history full of changes and fixes is no use to any detective.

Filtering the history with git log

You already know git log --oneline. These variations let you search for something specific:

QuestionCommand
What did Ana do?git log --oneline --author="Ana"
What changed this week?git log --oneline --since="1 week ago"
What changed between two dates?git log --oneline --since="2026-09-01" --until="2026-09-30"
Which commits touched this file?git log --oneline -- contact.md
Which commits mention "hours" in the message?git log --oneline --grep="hours"
Which commit added or removed this text?git log --oneline -S "500-0000"
How many files did each commit change?git log --stat

You can combine them. For example, Ana's commits that touched contact.md this month:

git log --oneline --author="Ana" --since="1 month ago" -- contact.md

The most powerful one for investigating errors is -S. It finds the commits where a text appeared or disappeared. If a phone number changed and nobody knows when, git log -S "old-number" takes you straight to the commit.

Seeing a file as it was before

To see a complete file exactly as it was in a commit, without changing anything in your folder:

git show a3f9c21:contact.md

It is like opening an old photo of the file. If what you want is to recover it, use git restore --source from chapter 7.

git blame: who wrote each line?

git blame contact.md

It shows the file line by line and, next to each one, the commit, the author and the date of the last change:

a3f9c21 (Ana Pérez  2026-10-01) Hours: Monday to Friday, 8:00 to 5:00
7b2e104 (Luis Gómez 2026-09-15) Phone: 500-0000

To see only some lines, for example lines 10 to 20:

git blame -L 10,20 contact.md

In GitLab you have the same thing more comfortably: open the file and press the Blame button. You can click each commit to see the full change.

About the name. Blame means "to blame" in English, but don't use it to hunt for culprits. Use it to find who to ask and to understand the context. The person who wrote that line is the best one to explain why it is like that. And that person may be you, six months ago.

Comparing versions and branches

git diff is not only for your unsaved changes. It also compares any pair of moments:

git diff a3f9c21 7b2e104                 # Between two commits
git diff main feature/form               # Between two branches
git diff main feature/form --stat        # Only which files change and how much
git diff main -- contact.md              # One file against main

To know which commits your branch has that main doesn't have yet:

git log --oneline main..feature/form

And the other way around, what main has that your branch is missing:

git log --oneline feature/form..main

If the second command shows many commits, it is a sign that your branch is falling behind and it is time to bring in main (chapter 4).

In GitLab, the equivalent option is under Code > Compare revisions: you choose two branches, tags or commits and see all the differences.

git fetch and git pull: look before you combine

Until now you have used git pull to bring in changes. Actually pull does two things in a row:

  1. git fetch: downloads the new information from GitLab, without touching your files or your branch.

  2. git merge: combines that information with your branch.

Sometimes it is good to do only the first part, to look before combining:

git fetch
git log --oneline main..origin/main     # What is new on GitLab?
git diff main origin/main --stat        # Which files changed?

origin/main is the copy your computer keeps of how main is on GitLab. After reviewing, if everything is fine, run git pull (or git merge origin/main).

It is like the mailbox from chapter 4: fetch brings the letters in, and you decide when to open them.

How branches are combined inside

In chapter 5 you saw the squash option. It is worth understanding the four ways Git can combine one branch with another, because each leaves a different history.

Fast-forward

If main hasn't changed since you created your branch, Git only has to move the label of main to your last commit. No new commit is created:

Before:   main: A --- B
                      \
         branch:       C --- D

After:    main: A --- B --- C --- D

Merge commit

If main did change, Git creates a special commit that joins the two lines. That commit has two "parents":

main: A --- B --- E ------- M
             \             /
branch:       C --- D ----

Advantage: the history shows exactly when work happened in parallel and when it was joined. Disadvantage: with many branches, the drawing becomes a tangle.

Squash

All the commits of your branch become one when they enter main:

main: A --- B --- E --- S     (S contains C + D together)

Advantage: main stays clean, one commit per task. Disadvantage: the detail of the intermediate steps is lost.

Rebase

Instead of joining the lines, Git "rewrites" your commits as if you had made them after the latest changes in main:

main: A --- B --- E --- C' --- D'

Advantage: a straight-line history, very easy to read. Disadvantage: it rewrites your commits (that is why they carry an apostrophe: they are new copies, with other identifiers).

The golden rule of rebase: never rebase commits that other people have already downloaded. If you do, your history and theirs stop matching and you cause them serious problems. Use it only on your own branch, before sharing it, and when you feel confident with everything above.

Which one to use?

Usually you don't decide this yourself: your team defines it in the project configuration on GitLab (Settings > Merge requests > Merge method). What matters is that you understand why your project's history looks the way it does.

MethodResulting historyCommon in
Merge commitShows branches and mergesTeams that want full traceability
SquashOne commit per task in mainTeams that prioritize a clean main
Fast-forward / rebaseStraight lineExperienced teams with short branches

Finding when an error started

When you know something worked before and doesn't now, you can search for the culprit commit step by step:

  1. Find a moment when it worked (an old commit or tag).

  2. Review the intermediate commits with git log --oneline good-commit..main.

  3. If there are few, read their messages and changes with git show.

  4. If there are many, test the project at a commit from the middle. Depending on whether it works or not, you will know which half has the error. Repeat.

Git automates this "split in half" method with a command called git bisect. We won't look at it in depth, but now you know it exists and what it is for: when you need it someday, the idea will already feel familiar.

Real example: the phone number that changed by itself

A user reports that the site's contact phone number is wrong. Nobody remembers changing it.

Ana searches for the correct number in the history:

git log --oneline -S "500-0000"

Two commits appear: the one that added the number months ago, and one from last week that removed it, with the message Update footer. Ana opens it with git show and sees that, while reorganizing the footer, an old phone number was copied by mistake.

With git blame she confirms that the current line comes from that commit. She writes to the person who made it, they figure out together what happened, and Ana corrects the number on a branch fix/contact-phone with her Merge Request. Everything in fifteen minutes and without blaming anyone.

Practical exercise

In your practice-git project, which by now has several commits:

  1. List only your commits from this week using --author and --since.

  2. Search with --grep for a commit whose word you remember.

  3. Use git log -S to find the commit that added a specific line of README.md.

  4. Open hours.md (from chapter 6) with git blame and then with GitLab's Blame button.

  5. Show how README.md looked in your first commit with git show code:README.md.

  6. Create a branch, make two commits and compare it with main using git diff main your-branch --stat and git log --oneline main..your-branch.

  7. Run git fetch and compare main with origin/main before running git pull.

  8. Run git log --oneline --graph --all and find a merge commit from the earlier chapters. How many parents does it have?

  9. Check in GitLab, under Settings > Merge requests, which merge method your project uses.

Chapter checklist

  • I can filter the history by author, date, file and message.

  • I can use git log -S to find the commit that added or removed a text.

  • I can view a file as it was in an earlier commit.

  • I use git blame to find context, not culprits.

  • I can compare commits and branches with git diff and git log a..b.

  • I understand that pull is fetch plus merge.

  • I know how to review what is new on GitLab before combining it.

  • I understand the difference between fast-forward, merge commit, squash and rebase.

  • I know the golden rule of rebase.

  • I know how to find when an error started by splitting the history.

PreviousChapter 11. Git with an Agent: You Decide, the AI ExecutesNextChapter 13. Versions, Tags and Forks

Chapter 13

Versions, Tags and Forks

Tags mark important moments in the project, like "this is version 1.0 that was published". Forks let you contribute to projects that aren't yours. And Markdown is the format you will write READMEs, Merge Requests, issues and the wiki in. These three tools complete your daily work with Git.

Why mark versions

Imagine the system your team maintains is published every two weeks. One day a user reports a bug and says: "this worked in last month's version". Which commit was that version?

If nobody marked it, you have to search through hundreds of commits by date and guess. If the team used tags, you write v1.3.0 and get there directly.

A tag is a fixed name given to a commit. Unlike a branch, it doesn't move: it always points to the same commit. It is like putting a bookmark on a page of the book.

How to number versions: semantic versioning

The most used convention is called semantic versioning. The number has three parts: MAJOR.MINOR.PATCH.

PartWhen it goes upExample
PATCHBug fixes, with no change in how it is used1.4.2 → 1.4.3
MINORNew features that don't break what came before1.4.3 → 1.5.0
MAJORChanges that force users to adapt1.5.0 → 2.0.0

When one part goes up, the ones to its right go back to zero. And versions that start with 0 (like 0.3.1) indicate the project is still in development and may change a lot.

By custom, tags carry a v in front: v1.5.0.

Creating and publishing tags

There are two types. Almost always use the annotated one, which stores the author, the date and a message:

git switch main
git pull
git tag -a v1.0.0 -m "First stable version of the site"

This tags the latest commit of main. To tag an earlier commit, add its code at the end: git tag -a v0.9.0 -m "…" 7b2e104.

Tags don't travel on their own with the push. You have to publish them:

git push origin v1.0.0     # One tag
git push origin --tags     # All local tags

Other useful commands:

CommandWhat it does
git tagLists the tags
git tag -l "v1.*"Lists only those starting with v1
git show v1.0.0Shows the tag and its commit
git switch --detach v1.0.0Takes you to see the project as it was in that version
git tag -d v1.0.0Deletes the tag on your computer
git push origin --delete v1.0.0Deletes the tag on GitLab

About --detach. When you go to a tag, Git warns you that you are in detached HEAD mode: you are not on any branch. You can look and try things, but if you make commits there they are left "loose". To return, run git switch main. If you need to fix something in that version, create a branch from it: git switch -c hotfix/v1.0.1 v1.0.0.

An important rule: don't change a tag that has already been published. If version v1.0.0 had a bug, the fix is v1.0.1. Moving a tag confuses everyone who already downloaded it.

Releases in GitLab

A release is a tag with presentation: a title, notes explaining what the version brings and, if needed, files to download. It is created in Deploy > Releases > New release (or from Code > Tags).

Good release notes answer what matters to whoever is going to use it:

## v1.5.0 — October 8, 2026

### New
- Contact form with email validation.
- Date filter in search.

### Fixed
- The footer phone number showed an old number.

### Breaking changes
- None. This version is compatible with the previous one.

Many projects also keep a CHANGELOG.md file at the root of the repository with these notes, from the newest version to the oldest. If your Merge Requests have good titles (chapter 5), writing the notes is almost copying and sorting.

Forks: contributing to projects that aren't yours

Until now you have worked on projects where you had permission to create branches. But what if you want to propose an improvement to a public project, or to one from another department, where you don't have write permission?

That is what the fork is for: a complete copy of the project in your own account on GitLab. In your copy you can do whatever you want. When you have something ready, you open a Merge Request from your fork to the original project. Its maintainers decide whether to accept it.

BranchFork
Where it livesInside the same projectIn your account, as a separate project
You need write permissionYesNo
When it is usedWork within your teamContributing to other people's or open source projects

The path of a contribution with a fork

  1. In the original project, press Fork and choose your account.

  2. Clone your fork, not the original:

git clone git@gitlab.com:your-username/project.git
cd project

  1. Also connect the original project, so you can bring in its updates. By custom it is called upstream:

git remote add upstream git@gitlab.com:original-team/project.git
git remote -v

Now you have two remotes: origin (your fork) and upstream (the original).

  1. Create a branch, make your changes and publish them to your fork:

git switch -c fix/documentation-error
# you make the changes and commits
git push -u origin fix/documentation-error

  1. In GitLab, open a Merge Request from your fork to the original project. Check the project and the target branch carefully.

  2. Respond to comments as in chapter 5: every push to your branch updates the Merge Request.

Keeping your fork up to date

The original project keeps moving forward. To bring its changes into your fork:

git switch main
git fetch upstream
git merge upstream/main
git push origin main

Do it before starting each new contribution, so you don't start from an old version.

Etiquette for contributing to other people's projects

  • Read the README and the CONTRIBUTING.md file first, if it exists. They explain how they prefer to receive changes.

  • For big changes, open an issue first and ask if they are interested. That way you don't spend days on something they won't accept.

  • Make small, well-described Merge Requests. The people who maintain open projects are often volunteers with little time.

  • Be patient. A reply can take days. A friendly reminder after a week is fine.

On GitHub it is called… The same: Fork. A Merge Request from a fork is a Pull Request.

Basic Markdown: the format you will write every day

Markdown is a simple way to format text using symbols. GitLab and GitHub turn it into titles, lists and links. You will use it in the README, Merge Request descriptions, comments, issues and the wiki. Files end in .md.

What you writeWhat you see
# TitleA large title
## SubtitleA medium title
**bold**bold
*italic*italic
- itemA bulleted list
1. stepA numbered list
- [ ] taskA checkbox to tick
[text](https://link.com)A link
`command`Code text inline
> noteA quote block
![description](image.png)An image

For multi-line code blocks, write three backticks before and after, optionally with the language:

```bash
git status
```

And tables are written with pipes and hyphens:

| Command | What for |
| --- | --- |
| git status | See the state |
| git log | See the history |

In GitLab, inside issues and Merge Requests, there are extra shortcuts that are very useful:

  • #42 links issue 42.

  • !15 links Merge Request 15.

  • @username mentions a person and sends them a notification.

  • Pasting a commit's code turns it into a link to that commit.

A good README

The README is the entry door to the project. This structure works for almost all of them:

# Project name

One or two sentences about what it does and for whom.

## How to start
Steps to download it and get it running.

## How to use it
Basic examples.

## How to contribute
How to name branches, how to open Merge Requests, who to ask.

## Contact
Project owners.

A clear README saves dozens of repeated questions for your team, and for yourself a year from now.

Real example: the version that had to be fixed

Ana's team publishes the site and tags the commit as v2.0.0, with a release listing what's new. Two days later a bug appears in the form.

Instead of mixing the fix with the new work already entering main, Ana creates a branch from the tag: git switch -c hotfix/v2.0.1 v2.0.0. She fixes it, opens her Merge Request, it is merged, and it is tagged v2.0.1 with a note: "Fixed: the form didn't accept emails with a hyphen".

Weeks later, someone asks since when that fix exists. The answer is in Deploy > Releases, without searching through commits.

Practical exercise

  1. In practice-git, update main and create an annotated tag v0.1.0 with the message "First practice version". Publish it.

  2. Make a new commit on a branch, merge it with a Merge Request and create the tag v0.2.0.

  3. In GitLab, create a release for v0.2.0 with release notes in Markdown, using the New and Fixed sections.

  4. Use git switch --detach v0.1.0 to see how the project was. Return with git switch main.

  5. Rewrite the README.md of your project with this chapter's structure. Include at least a title, a list, a link, a code block and a table. See how it looks in GitLab.

  6. Find a small public project in GitLab or GitHub and make a fork. Clone it, add upstream and check both remotes with git remote -v. You don't need to send a contribution yet: read its README and its CONTRIBUTING.md and note how they prefer to receive changes.

Chapter checklist

  • I know what a tag is and how it differs from a branch.

  • I understand semantic versioning: MAJOR.MINOR.PATCH.

  • I know how to create an annotated tag and publish it.

  • I know how to view the project at an earlier version and return to main.

  • I never move a tag that has been published: I release a new version instead.

  • I know how to create a release in GitLab with good release notes.

  • I know what a fork is and when to use it instead of a branch.

  • I know how to connect upstream and keep my fork up to date.

  • I read the README and CONTRIBUTING before contributing to someone else's project.

  • I know how to write basic Markdown: titles, lists, links, code and tables.

  • I know how to use #, ! and @ in GitLab to link issues, Merge Requests and people.

  • My practice project has a clear README.

PreviousChapter 12. Investigating History: Git as a DetectiveNextChapter 14. Final Practical Project

Chapter 14

Final Practical Project

In this chapter you will go through the complete teamwork flow, from start to finish, as you would in a real project: clone, create an issue and a branch, make changes, open a Merge Request, fix a comment, check the pipeline and complete the merge. There are no new topics. It is time to put everything you learned together.

The scenario

Your team maintains a small internal guide for new people: a page with the welcome, the team's contacts and the frequently asked questions. You are asked to add a new section of frequently asked questions about Git, because the people who arrive always ask the same things.

It is a small change on purpose. What matters is not the content, but doing each step of the process well.

How to do it: in pairs, or alone

In pairs (recommended). One person is the author and the other the reviewer. When you finish, you swap roles and repeat with another task. That way you live both sides of the Merge Request.

Alone. You play both roles. For the review, leave a few hours between writing and reviewing: you will see your work with fresher eyes. If you can, ask someone with a GitLab account to do only the review.

Project setup

You need a project that has a pipeline, to practice reading it. There are three paths, in this order of preference:

  1. Your organization or your instructor gives you a project already prepared, with a pipeline. Use it.

  2. Create it from a GitLab template that already includes a pipeline. In New project > Create from template, choose a simple website template, such as Pages/Plain HTML. You don't have to configure anything: the .gitlab-ci.yml file is already included.

  3. Use your practice-git project. It won't have a pipeline, so in phase 6 you will only verify that there is none and review how it would be read.

On GitLab.com, for pipelines to run, the platform may ask you to verify your account. If you can't or don't want to do it, follow path 3: the rest of the project works the same way.

We will call the project team-guide. If you are the owner, add your partner as Developer (chapter 10) and protect main: merge only by Maintainers, direct push No one.

Phase 1: clone the project

Goal: have the project on your computer, up to date.

cd Documents
git clone git@gitlab.com:your-username/team-guide.git
cd team-guide
git status
git log --oneline -5

Checkpoint:

  • git status says you are on main and everything is clean.

  • You read the README to understand the project.

  • git config user.email shows the email of your GitLab account.

If something fails: check the table of common problems in chapter 2.

Phase 2: create the issue and the branch

Goal: have the task exist in GitLab before touching the code.

  1. In GitLab, create an issue (chapter 8):

Title: Add frequently asked questions about Git to the guide

## What is needed
A section with frequently asked questions about Git for new people.

## Why
People who arrive always ask the same questions.

## When we consider it done
- [ ] There are at least five questions with their answers.
- [ ] The section is linked from the home page.
- [ ] The text has no spelling mistakes.

  1. Add the label documentation, assign it to yourself and, if you have one, add it to a milestone.

  2. Note its number. In the examples we will use #7.

  3. Create the branch. You can use the Create merge request button of the issue, or do it from the terminal:

git switch main
git pull
git switch -c docs/7-git-faq

Checkpoint:

  • The issue has a clear title, description and done criteria.

  • The branch follows the convention type/number-description.

  • You started from an updated main.

  • You moved the issue to In progress on the board, if you use one.

Phase 3: make the changes and the commits

Goal: small changes, reviewed before saving, with clear messages.

  1. Create the file for the section. If the project is in HTML, create git-faq.html; if it is in Markdown, git-faq.md. Write at least five questions with short answers. Ideas: "What is a commit?", "Why don't I work on main?", "What do I do if a conflict appears?", "What is a Merge Request?", "What do I do if I uploaded a password?".

  2. Look before you save and make the first commit:

git status
git diff
git add git-faq.md
git diff --staged
git commit -m "Add frequently asked questions section about Git (#7)"

  1. Now link the section from the home page (the index.html or the README.md). It is a different idea, so it goes in another commit:

git add index.html
git commit -m "Link frequently asked questions from the home page (#7)"

  1. Review your history and publish the branch:

git log --oneline -3
git push -u origin docs/7-git-faq

Checkpoint:

  • You made at least two commits, one per idea.

  • Each message starts with a verb and mentions the issue.

  • You used git status and git diff before each commit.

  • No file unrelated to the task got in.

  • The branch appears in GitLab, under Code > Branches.

Optional: if you practice with an agent, do this phase with it following the rules of chapter 11, and verify everything manually.

Phase 4: open the Merge Request

Goal: ask for review with all the information the reviewer needs.

  1. Open the Merge Request from the link the push gave you.

  2. Verify: source docs/7-git-faq, target main.

  3. Title: Add frequently asked questions about Git to the guide.

  4. Description, using the chapter 5 template:

## What changes
Adds a section of frequently asked questions about Git and links it
from the home page.

## Why
New people always ask the same questions.

## How to test it
1. Open the home page.
2. Click "Frequently asked questions about Git".
3. Check that the questions and answers appear.

Closes #7

  1. Assign your partner as Reviewer, add the label documentation and tick Delete source branch.

  2. Before announcing it, review the whole Changes tab yourself.

Checkpoint:

  • Source and target are correct.

  • The description explains what, why and how to test.

  • It includes Closes #7.

  • You reviewed your own changes before asking for review.

Phase 5: review and fix a comment

Goal: live the cycle of comment, fix and resolution.

If you are the reviewer:

  1. Read the description and then the Changes tab.

  2. Leave at least two comments with Start a review: one marked as a suggestion, using Insert suggestion, and another that asks for a real change (for example, an answer that could be explained better, or a missing question).

  3. Remember: comments about the content, with the reason, and something positive if there is any.

  4. Submit the review.

If you are the author:

  1. Read each comment in full.

  2. Apply the suggestion with Apply suggestion.

  3. For the other comment, make the fix on your computer. Since you applied a suggestion in GitLab, first bring that change in:

git pull
# you make the fix
git status
git diff
git add git-faq.md
git commit -m "Clarify the answer about conflicts (#7)"
git push

  1. Verify that the Merge Request updated itself with your new commit.

  2. Reply to each thread with what you did and resolve them (or let the person who commented resolve them, according to your team's agreement).

  3. Request the review again.

Extra challenge: a conflict. While the Merge Request is open, have the other person make a small Merge Request that changes the same line with the link on the home page, and merge it first. Your Merge Request will show a conflict. Resolve it on your computer with git merge main (chapter 6), keeping both changes, and push.

Checkpoint:

  • There were at least two comments and both were addressed.

  • The fix arrived as a new commit on the same branch.

  • All threads were resolved.

  • If you did the challenge, the conflict was resolved without losing any change.

Phase 6: check the pipeline

Goal: read the traffic light before merging (chapter 9). Remember: you won't configure anything, only read.

  1. In the Merge Request, find the pipeline box. Each push you made ran it automatically.

  2. If it is running, wait.

  3. If it is passed, open the pipeline and look at its stages and jobs. Open the log of a job and read the last lines.

  4. If it is failed, apply the process from chapter 9: identify the job, read the end of the log, relate it to your change, fix it and push. If it has nothing to do with your change, use Retry once; if it still fails, ask for help with the link and the error lines.

  5. If your project has no pipeline (path 3), you will see no box. Go to Build > Pipelines and confirm it is empty. Then review, in a public project, how it would look.

Checkpoint:

  • I know what state the pipeline ended in and why.

  • I did not move on to the merge with the pipeline red.

Phase 7: complete the merge

Goal: close the cycle and leave everything tidy.

  1. Review the "What to check before merging" list from chapter 10.

  2. The reviewer approves with Approve.

  3. Whoever has merge permission presses Merge.

  4. Verify in GitLab:

    • Issue #7 closed on its own.

    • The branch was deleted.

    • In Code > Commits of main your commits appear (or just one, if squash was used).

    • The board and the milestone were updated.

  5. Update your computer:

git switch main
git pull
git branch -d docs/7-git-faq
git fetch --prune
git log --oneline --graph -10

  1. Optional: create the tag v1.1.0 with its release and release notes (chapter 13).

Checkpoint:

  • The Merge Request was merged with approval and with everything resolved.

  • The issue closed automatically.

  • Your local main is up to date and the local branch was deleted.

Phase 8: switch roles

If you work in pairs, repeat everything with the roles reversed and a different task. Ideas:

  • Add a section with the team's contacts.

  • Fix spelling mistakes on the home page (a fix/ type).

  • Create the page "How we work" in the wiki and link it from the README.

Final assessment

Review your work with this table. If something ended up as "not yet", go back to the indicated chapter.

SkillI got itNot yet: review
Clone and configure my identityChapter 2
Create a clear issue with done criteriaChapter 8
Create a branch with the right convention from an updated mainChapter 4
Make small, reviewed commits with good messagesChapter 3
Open a well-described Merge RequestChapter 5
Review and comment respectfullyChapter 5
Fix comments on the same branchChapter 5
Resolve a conflict without losing workChapter 6
Read the pipeline statusChapter 9
Merge with all the protections and clean up afterwardChapters 5 and 10
Verify in GitLab a change made with an agent's helpChapter 11

Questions to reflect on

When you finish, answer in writing. It will help you fix what you learned:

  1. Which step of the process cost you the most, and why?

  2. Which review comment improved your work the most?

  3. If there was a conflict, how did you decide what to keep?

  4. What would you do differently next time?

  5. Which rule from this book will you propose to your team first?

Congratulations

You have just done, from start to finish, the same flow that technology teams follow every day all over the world. You are no longer someone who "doesn't know Git": you are someone who can work in a team with version control. What comes next is practice. The 7-day plan helps you turn it into a habit.

PreviousChapter 13. Versions, Tags and ForksNext7-Day Action Plan

Closing

7-Day Action Plan

This plan turns the book into daily practice. Each day has a goal, concrete tasks, an approximate time and a small test to find out whether you got it. Spend between one and two hours a day. If a day doesn't fit, nothing happens: continue the next day from where you left off. What matters is not skipping steps.

Day 1: get everything ready and understand the why

Goal: have Git ready and understand what problem it solves.

Chapters: 0, 1 and 2. Time: 1 to 2 hours.

  • Read chapters 0 and 1.

  • Review a folder of yours full of manual versions (exercise from chapter 0).

  • Install Git and configure your name and email.

  • Create your GitLab account and set up SSH (or a token).

  • Create and clone your practice-git project.

Test of the day: explain to someone, in two minutes and without technical words, what Git is and what GitLab is.

Day 2: the basic cycle

Goal: save and publish changes, always looking first.

Chapter: 3. Time: 1 to 2 hours.

  • Make at least five small commits in practice-git, each one with git status and git diff before.

  • Create your .gitignore.

  • Practice git add -p on a file with two different changes.

  • Publish your commits and find them in GitLab.

  • Fix a message with git commit --amend before pushing.

Test of the day: write from memory, in order, the commands of the cycle: status, diff, add, commit, log, pull, push.

Day 3: branches and Merge Requests

Goal: work without touching main and ask for review.

Chapters: 4 and 5. Time: 2 hours.

  • Create two branches with the convention type/description.

  • Bring main into one of them with git merge main.

  • Open a Merge Request with the description template.

  • Comment on a line, use a suggestion and resolve the thread.

  • Merge, update main and delete the local branch.

Test of the day: without looking at the book, go through the full path of a new branch until it is merged.

Day 4: conflicts and mistakes

Goal: lose your fear of conflicts and of making mistakes.

Chapters: 6 and 7. Time: 2 hours.

  • Cause a conflict on purpose and resolve it by combining both changes.

  • Cancel a merge with git merge --abort.

  • Do the full first-aid exercise: restore, reset, stash, revert and reflog.

  • Write your personal first-aid sheet.

Test of the day: answer without looking: "I already pushed a commit with a mistake. Which command do I use, and why don't I use reset?".

Day 5: organization, pipelines and security

Goal: work like on a real team, with order and protections.

Chapters: 8, 9 and 10. Time: 2 hours.

  • Create labels, a milestone and three issues with templates.

  • Create a branch from an issue and close it with Closes #number.

  • Read a failed pipeline in a public project and explain the error.

  • Protect main in your project and check that you can't push to it directly.

  • Create .env and .env.example, and turn on two-step verification.

Test of the day: go through in GitLab the full chain of a task: issue, branch, commits, Merge Request and closed issue.

Day 6: extra tools and automation

Goal: investigate the history, mark versions and use an agent with control.

Chapters: 11, 12 and 13. Time: 2 hours.

  • Use git log -S and git blame to investigate a change.

  • Create a tag v0.1.0 and a release with notes.

  • Improve your README with Markdown.

  • Write the rules for your agent or prepare the publish.sh script.

  • Make a full change with the help of the agent or the script, and verify it manually in GitLab.

Test of the day: explain five things you would never let an agent do on its own.

Day 7: final project and agreements

Goal: do the whole flow from start to finish and write your rules down.

Chapter: 14. Time: 2 to 3 hours.

  • Complete the seven phases of the final project, preferably in pairs.

  • Fill in the final assessment table and review what ended up as "not yet".

  • Answer the reflection questions.

  • Write the page "Team rules" in the wiki, with your agreements on branches, commits, reviews and security.

Test of the day: the final project was merged, with the issue closed and no work lost.

After the 7 days: habits for every working day

What you learned is kept by using it. These habits turn the book into your way of working:

When you start the day

  • git switch main and git pull.

  • Review the board and choose your task.

  • Create the branch from the issue.

During the day

  • Small commits, looking first with git status and git diff.

  • Bring main into your branch if the work drags on.

  • Comment progress and questions on the issue.

When you finish the day

  • Push your branch, even if the work isn't finished: that way it is backed up.

  • Open the Merge Request as a draft if you need an early opinion.

  • Leave your folder clean: nothing half-done without a commit or a named stash.

Every week

  • Review your open Merge Requests and the ones you have to review.

  • Delete local branches already merged and run git fetch --prune.

  • Learn one new GitLab command or feature and try it in practice-git.

If at any point you feel lost, go back to the command sheet that comes next. And remember the promise from the beginning: almost everything in Git can be undone.

Closing

Pocket Glossary

All the technical words in the book, explained in one line, in alphabetical order. The number indicates the chapter where it is explained in depth.

TermWhat it meansCh.
ApproveGive the green light to a Merge Request after reviewing it5
Staging areaThe "box" where you put what goes into the next commit3
BlameA view showing who last changed each line of a file12
BranchA parallel line of work to make changes without touching the main one4
CI/CDAutomatic checks and tasks that run with each change9
CloneDownloading a project from GitLab to your computer, with its whole history2
CommitA save point of the project, with a message explaining the change1 and 3
ConflictWhen two changes touch the same part and Git asks you to decide6
DiffThe list of differences between two versions, line by line3
DraftA Merge Request that is not yet ready to be merged5
.envLocal file with configuration and secrets, which is never uploaded10
LabelA colored mark to classify issues and Merge Requests8
TagA fixed name for an important commit, such as v1.0.013
Fast-forwardA merge where only the branch moves forward, with no new commit12
FetchBringing new information from GitLab without combining it with your work4 and 12
ForkA copy of someone else's project in your account, to propose changes13
GitThe program that keeps the history of changes of a project1
.gitignoreFile listing what Git must ignore3
GitHubA web platform for sharing repositories, similar to GitLab1
GitLabA web platform for sharing, reviewing and organizing repositories1
HEADThe commit you are on right now7
HistoryThe list of all the commits of the project1
IssueA card for a task, bug or request, with its own number8
JobA specific task inside a pipeline9
mainCommon name of the main branch4
MaintainerGitLab role that can merge on protected branches10
MarkdownA simple text format with symbols for titles, lists and links13
MergeCombining the changes of one branch with another4 and 5
Merge Request (MR)A request to review and merge a branch. On GitHub: Pull Request5
MilestoneA dated goal that groups issues and Merge Requests8
originNickname of the copy of the project on GitLab2
PipelineThe sequence of automatic checks for a change9
PullBringing changes from GitLab and combining them with your branch3
Pull Request (PR)The name of the Merge Request on GitHub5
PushSending your commits to GitLab3
Protected branchA branch with rules about who can change it and how10
READMEThe introduction file of the project1 and 13
RebaseRewriting your commits on top of the newest version of another branch12
ReflogA record of all your movements, to recover commits7
ReleaseA published version with a title and notes13
RemoteThe copy of the repository on GitLab1
Repository (repo)The project folder with its whole history1
ResetMoving your branch backward, undoing local commits7
RestoreReturning files to a saved version7
RevertA new commit that cancels an earlier commit7
RunnerA machine that runs the jobs of a pipeline9
SecretA password, token or key that gives access to something10
SquashCombining several commits into one when merging5 and 12
SSHA secure way to connect to GitLab with a pair of keys2
StageA group of jobs in a pipeline that run together9
StashA temporary drawer to set aside half-finished changes7
BoardA view of issues as cards in columns8
TerminalA window where you give orders to the computer by typing2
TokenA special key with limited permissions, which replaces your password2
upstreamNickname of the original project when you work with a fork13
WikiA documentation space inside the project8

Command Cheat Sheet

The commands of the book grouped by situation. Those marked with ⚠ can delete work: check with git status and git log before using them.

Setup (one time only)

CommandWhat for
git --versionCheck that Git is installed
git config --global user.name "Name"Set your name
git config --global user.email "email"Set your email
git config --global init.defaultBranch mainMake the main branch be called main
git config --global --listView your configuration
ssh-keygen -t ed25519 -C "email"Create your SSH keys
ssh -T git@gitlab.comTest the connection with GitLab

Starting a project

CommandWhat for
git clone addressDownload a project from GitLab
git initTurn a folder into a repository
git remote add origin addressConnect your repository with GitLab
git remote -vSee which remotes it is connected to

The everyday cycle

CommandWhat for
git statusSee what changed and which branch you are on
git diffSee the changes that are not in the box
git diff --stagedSee what is already in the box
git add filePut a file in the box
git add -p fileChoose parts of a file
git restore --staged fileTake a file out of the box
git commit -m "Message"Save a commit
git commit --amendFix the last commit (only before push)
git log --onelineView the history in summary
git show codeView a commit in detail
git pullBring in and combine changes from GitLab
git pushPublish your commits

Branches

CommandWhat for
git branchSee your branches
git branch -aAlso see the GitLab branches
git switch -c type/nameCreate a branch and enter it
git switch nameSwitch branches
git push -u origin namePublish a new branch
git merge mainBring the changes of main into your branch
git branch -d nameDelete a merged branch
⚠ git branch -D nameDelete an unmerged branch
git push origin --delete nameDelete a branch on GitLab
git fetch --pruneClean up references to deleted branches
git log --oneline --graph --allSee the branches drawn

Conflicts

CommandWhat for
git statusSee which files have a conflict
git merge --abortCancel the merge and return to how it was
git add fileMark a conflict as resolved
git commitFinish the merge

Undoing

CommandWhat for
⚠ git restore fileDiscard changes not committed
git restore --source code fileRecover a file from an earlier commit
git revert codeCancel a published commit
git reset --soft HEAD~1Undo the last commit, keeping everything in the box
git reset HEAD~1Undo the last commit, keeping the changes
⚠ git reset --hard HEAD~1Undo the last commit and delete its changes
git stash push -m "note"Set aside changes in progress
git stash listSee what you have set aside
git stash popRecover what was set aside
git reflogSee all your movements to recover commits
⚠ git clean -fDelete files Git doesn't track (use -n first)

Investigating

CommandWhat for
git log --oneline --author="Name"A person's commits
git log --oneline --since="1 week ago"Recent commits
git log --oneline -- fileCommits that touched a file
git log --oneline --grep="word"Search the messages
git log -S "text"Find where a text appeared or disappeared
git blame fileWho changed each line
git diff main branchDifferences between two branches
git log --oneline main..branchCommits a branch has and main doesn't
git fetchBring in news without combining it

Versions and forks

CommandWhat for
git tag -a v1.0.0 -m "Message"Create a version tag
git push origin v1.0.0Publish a tag
git tagSee the tags
git switch --detach v1.0.0See the project at an earlier version
git remote add upstream addressConnect the original project of a fork
git fetch upstream and git merge upstream/mainUpdate your fork

Never on shared branches

CommandWhy
⚠ git push --forceRewrites the team's history and can delete other people's work
⚠ git rebase on published commitsChanges commits that others have already downloaded
⚠ git reset --hard on published commitsYour branch stops matching the team's
